Professional APEX
The Deployment Guide
Three-Layer Deployment Strategy on Microsoft Platform
Volume III · Deployment
From Lab to Multi-Tenant Production

A field manual for deploying Services and Agents on the Microsoft Platform via the APEX framework, across multiple Azure tenants — anchored on the MVP as a reusable framework asset for accelerating RC-E2E-03 delivery and expanding to the broader 38-Service catalog. APEX is the framework that accelerates Service delivery; what's deployed in client tenants are Services with KPI envelopes. The closing volume of the APEX Trilogy — paired with the Sellers Guide (Volume I · positioning APEX-accelerated Services) and the Services Guide (Volume II · building Services on the APEX framework).

WROX-STYLE EDITION · DEPLOYMENT VOLUME
Keven Markham · VP Enterprise Transformation · Deloitte's Microsoft Technology & Services Practice · May 2026

How to Use This Book

What APEX Is — read this first

APEX is a framework that accelerates the building of services and agents on the Microsoft Platform. It is the architectural substrate Deloitte's Microsoft practice uses internally to deliver Services faster, more consistently, and with more rigorous governance. APEX is not a product and is not sold to clients. What clients buy are Services — productized envelopes from the 38-Service catalog (RC-E2E-03 · HLS-E2E-04 · TMT-TEL-RAA-04 · etc.), each with a KPI commitment and a two-contract Independence-compliant commercial structure (client ↔ Microsoft for platform · client ↔ Deloitte for the Service-delivery engagement).

What this means for deployment leads: the three-layer cake (Platform · Service · Agent) — the organizing idea below — is the framework's deployment topology. The Platform layer is the per-tenant Microsoft platform substrate Deloitte stands up under the framework's Bicep templates. The Service layer is the productized envelope the client contracted for; this is what gets deployed into the client tenant. The Agent layer is the reusable image set the framework promotes through the two-Container-Registry mirror. What's deployed in client tenants are Services and the agents that compose them, on the Microsoft Platform, accelerated by the APEX framework. When this book talks about "deploying APEX," read "deploying a Service that the framework built." The framework is the means; the Service is what's contracted for and what runs in the tenant.

This is the operator's companion to the Professional APEX Sellers Guide. Where the Sellers Guide answers how to position APEX-accelerated Services, this volume answers how to deploy them on the Microsoft Platform. It exists because, between a working laptop demo and a multi-tenant production rollout, there is a long list of questions that sellers, architects, and delivery leads keep asking — and a stable set of answers we have been refining since the MVP shipped.

The book is built around one organizing idea: the APEX framework deploys Services in three layers — Platform, Service, and Agent — and each layer has its own lifecycle, its own container registry, its own Bicep module set, and its own promotion path. If you keep the layers straight, the rest of deployment becomes routine. If you blur them, every onboarding becomes bespoke and every upgrade becomes risky. The whole book is a long argument for keeping the layers straight.

Audience

  • Delivery leads sequencing client onboardings — read Part I and Part V end-to-end, skim Parts II–IV.
  • Platform architects standing up Layer 1 in a new Azure tenant — Part II is the core; Appendix A is your Bicep reference.
  • Service owners for any of the 38 cataloged services (RC-E2E-03 through TMT-TEL-RAA-04) — Part III, plus Appendix C and Appendix E.
  • Agent maintainers shipping container images that traverse from Deloitte authoring registry to client-tenant runtime — Part IV and Appendix D.
  • Sellers who need to answer "is this productionizable?" — Chapters 1, 7, 13, and 16, plus the Independence callouts throughout.

How the Book Is Organized

Part I — Foundations of Deployment. The three-layer cake, what "substrate-aware" means in practice, and how Independence shapes the deployment posture (two contracts, no co-mingled ECIF, no "alliance" language).

Part II — Layer 1: APEX Platform. The shared substrate per tenant: Container Apps Environment, Premium container registry with Notary trust, four user-assigned managed identities, Premium HSM Key Vault with CMK, Log Analytics, App Insights. This is what every Service in that tenant runs on top of.

Part III — Layer 2: Service. A Service is a productized envelope (KPI · scope · two-contract commercial) that composes one or more agents over Platform. RC-E2E-03 (Pricing & Revenue Decision Service) is the running example. The 38-service catalog is enumerated in Appendix E.

Part IV — Layer 3: Agent. Container images that implement individual agents (The Analyst, The Demand Checker, The Finance Lead, The Operations Lead, The Briefer). Reusable across Services. Versioned independently. Signed with cosign and pulled by digest.

Part V — The Deployment Motion. The four motions you will run, in this order: stand up the Platform in a new Azure tenant; add a Service; upgrade an agent across multiple Services or tenants; recover from a bad upgrade.

Independence — read this first

This guide assumes the two-contract commercial model: client contracts Microsoft for the platform; client contracts Deloitte separately for services. ECIF flows direct from Microsoft to the client (not through Deloitte). Throughout the book, the relationship is described as "Deloitte's Microsoft practice," "DMTSP," "Microsoft platform capabilities," or "Microsoft-native deployment." The terms partner, alliance, strategic alliance do not appear and must not appear in any client-facing artifact derived from this material. If you are about to write one of those words in a deployment runbook, statement of work, or onboarding email — stop and reread Chapter 3.

Conventions

Callouts you will see repeatedly:

  • Note — clarifications and pointers.
  • Warning — common mistakes that have actually broken deployments.
  • Best Practice — what we have found works after the third or fourth tenant.
  • Try It Out / Key Play — concrete steps you can run on your laptop or against a Lab tenant.
  • Objection — pushback from CIOs, security teams, or procurement, with the answer.
  • Independence — language and structural reminders for the two-contract model.

Code blocks are labeled by language. Bicep, PowerShell, bash, YAML, and SQL all appear. File paths use forward slashes; on Windows, swap to backslashes if your shell needs it.

Note — A word on "ACR"

This book spells out Azure Container Registry (or container registry) in prose rather than using the abbreviation ACR. Microsoft itself uses ACR for Azure Container Registry — the az acr CLI family and the apex-acr.azurecr.io hostname are Microsoft's own naming and are kept as-is — but in Microsoft commercial contexts, ACR also means Azure Consumption Revenue. Because this guide sits next to the Sellers Guide and discusses ECIF and the two-contract model, we disambiguate by spelling the registry term out everywhere except in literal commands, hostnames, filenames, and Microsoft-defined RBAC role names (e.g., AcrPull).

Source Material and Cross-References

This guide is a book; it is not the source of truth for any particular component. The sources are:

  • MVP/docs/deployment.md — component inventory across the four substrate tiers (laptop · DEV · STAGE · PROD) and the Bicep module map per sprint.
  • MVP/docs/commercial/RC-E2E-03-service-envelope.md — the canonical Service example, with KPI envelope and two-scenario scope.
  • mvp-scope-q1-orchestrator.md — Q1 sprint plan: Sprint 1 Foundation+Analyst through Sprint 6 Lab v0.1 deployment.
  • APEX-Scenario-Chains.xlsx — 38 unique service IDs across 7 practices, source for Appendix E.
  • Professional-APEX-M-Sellers-Guide.html — the commercial companion volume; this guide assumes you have read at minimum its Chapters 2 (envelopes) and 6 (LEDGER).

When this guide gives a number, a Bicep parameter name, or a checklist item, the sources above are authoritative. Where they disagree with this book, they win. We will reconcile in the next edition.

Welcome to the Deployment Guide. Turn the page.

Part I

Foundations of Deployment

It's Day 0. Priya — lead deployment engineer at Deloitte — has just been handed her next engagement. The SOW is signed. The client is a Pacific Northwest grocery chain that already runs Microsoft Dynamics 365 and Microsoft Fabric in their own Azure tenant. The deliverable on the Wave-1 schedule: first Marisol-equivalent operator approves a real cold-chain markdown card in their own tenant within 90 days. Priya has read the Services Guide. She knows what Marisol will eventually see. Now her job is to build the platform that makes that card possible.

Before any Bicep module runs and before any container image is signed, there is a mental model to settle. APEX deploys in three layers: the Platform layer (Layer 1) is the long-lived Microsoft Azure foundation — the resource group, the Container Registry, the managed environment, Microsoft Entra ID identities, Key Vault, Log Analytics. The Service layer (Layer 2) is the per-Service Bicep that sits on top — KPI envelope, scope, MCP host, Gold marts, the orchestration substrate. The Agent layer (Layer 3) is the container images themselves — built once, signed with Microsoft Notation, pulled by SHA digest. Each layer has its own lifecycle. None of them re-deploys when another one changes.

The Deployment Guide is Priya's playbook from blank-subscription to first-card. Chapter 0 sets the demo-to-deployable mindset. Chapter 1 walks the three-layer cake — how the layers nest, why they're separable, what each owns. Chapter 2 covers substrate-aware architecture: the same container image runs on Priya's Docker Desktop laptop and on Microsoft.App managed environments in Azure without rebuilding. Chapter 3 nails the Independence posture — two contracts (client↔Microsoft for the platform; client↔Deloitte for the engagement), no shared ECIF, no co-mingled marketing. Once those four foundations are settled, the rest of the book has somewhere stable to stand.

Chapter 0

Foreword — From Demo to Deployable

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Frames why deployment is the bottleneck right now, not the demo or the agent quality.
  • Explains the MVP's role as a reusable asset — built once for RC-E2E-03, instrumented to expand across the 38-service catalog.
  • Establishes the three operational truths that shape every later chapter.
60-second read
  • Deployment, not agent quality, is now the bottleneck — what this book addresses.
  • The MVP is a reusable framework asset built around RC-E2E-03 and engineered to generalize across all 38 cataloged Services.
  • Three operational truths: three layers each with their own lifecycle · same image across two substrates · Independence is structural.
  • Plus the §0.5 design goal: zero-cost laptop development.

0.1 The Bottleneck Is No Longer the Agent

Two years ago the question was "can an LLM-driven agent reliably make a markdown decision a category manager will trust?" That question is now answered. The Sellers Guide documents the KPI envelope: Gross Margin +3.2pp, days-on-hand −28%, markdown-to-clear ratio +41%, $142K in margin protected per cold-chain excursion event, decision-loop time compressed from 5.7 hours to under 90 seconds with audit attribution. These are not laboratory numbers; they were earned in the W2 pilots that fed the MVP.

The bottleneck moved. Sellers can show a working agent on a laptop in twelve minutes. What they cannot show, easily, is the path from that laptop to a client's Azure tenant under a two-contract commercial model with cosign-signed images, customer-managed keys, an immutable audit ledger, and a Bicep deployment that another delivery team can run end-to-end in a different tenant next month. That is what this book is about.

0.2 The MVP Is a Reusable Asset, Not a Pilot

The Q1 MVP — built across six sprints in the Consumer/Merch/MVP repository — was scoped deliberately around RC-E2E-03 (Pricing & Revenue Decision Service). One service, two scenarios (cold-chain excursion, dynamic markdown), five agents (The Analyst, The Demand Checker, The Finance Lead, The Operations Lead, The Briefer), one substrate that runs on Docker Desktop on a delivery lead's laptop and on Azure Container Apps in the Lab. That is the smallest closed loop that proves the entire deployment motion.

The reason it was scoped that way is that everything in the MVP — the Bicep modules, the agent base image, the LEDGER hash chain, the test harness, the topology UX — is engineered to generalize. RC-E2E-03 is the test driver for an asset that is supposed to accelerate every subsequent service, from RC-E2E-04 (Loyalty churn) through TMT-TEL-RAA-04 (Monthly revenue assurance variance close). Appendix E lists all 38.

Note

If you ever hear "the MVP is just for retail" or "we'll rebuild this for healthcare," push back. The agent images, the Platform Bicep, and the LEDGER are industry-neutral. The schemas, fixtures, and KPI definitions per Service are what change. The asset is reusable; the Service is what gets reskinned.

0.3 The Three Operational Truths

Every chapter in this book is downstream of three operational truths. State them out loud once a week and you will avoid most of the deployment errors we have seen.

  1. Three layers, three lifecycles. Platform, Service, and Agent each have their own version, their own container registry, their own deployment cadence. Mixing them collapses lifecycles into a single brittle pipeline. Keeping them apart is the entire point of Chapters 1 and 12.
  2. Same image, two substrates. The container image that runs on a delivery lead's Docker Desktop is the same image that runs in Azure Container Apps. Substrate awareness lives at the edges — environment variables and Bicep parameters — not inside the agent code. Chapter 2 makes this concrete.
  3. Independence is structural, not stylistic. "DMTSP," "Microsoft platform capabilities," and "Microsoft-native deployment" are not preferences; they reflect a two-contract commercial model that the deployment architecture must enforce. Chapter 3 connects the language to the wiring.

0.4 What This Book Is Not

This is a deployment book. It is not:

  • A commercial book — that is the Sellers Guide. The KPI envelopes, ECIF eligibility, and Wave model live there.
  • An agent-design book — agent prompts, MCP tool catalogs, and persona briefs live in the Agentic-Merch component architecture and the runner-level documentation.
  • A data book — bronze/silver/gold schemas and the SCML/MERML/PROML data models are documented in the Data & Platform reference.

If you find yourself asking "what does this Service sell?" you want the Sellers Guide. If you are asking "how does this Service deploy?" you are in the right book.

0.5 Zero-Cost Development as a Design Goal

The fourth operational truth — a corollary to the three in §0.3 — is a design goal: the developer's inner loop runs at zero Azure cost. A new developer should be able to clone the repository, run docker compose up, exercise the entire MVP — agents, orchestration, LEDGER, harness, topology UX — and ship a working change without ever provisioning an Azure subscription or burning a dollar of consumption.

This is not a cost-optimization story; it is a structural choice that shapes substrate selection. Every laptop-tier component is free, open-source, and self-hostable in docker-compose:

  • Database — Azure Database for PostgreSQL on Azure → Postgres container locally (free).
  • Orchestration — Azure Logic Apps on Azure → n8n container locally (free, self-hosted under n8n's Sustainable Use License).
  • Agent runtime — Microsoft.App/containerApps on Azure → docker-compose locally (free).
  • Identity — Microsoft Entra ID on Azure → no-op stub locally (free).
  • Secrets — Microsoft.KeyVault on Azure → .env with synthetic fixtures locally (free).
  • Observability — Azure Monitor on Azure → stdout / file logs locally (free).

The first time a developer needs to think about cost is at the DEV (Lab v0.1) substrate transition, not during dev-loop iteration. The orchestration substrate parity in §2.6 — n8n on laptop, Azure Logic Apps on Azure — is the most consequential of these choices because it preserves zero-cost dev and Microsoft-platform-native production.

Best Practice — Preserve the zero-cost loop

If a new dependency would force the laptop tier to require an Azure subscription, treat it as a structural change. Push the dependency to DEV+ substrates and stub it on laptop. The zero-cost guarantee is what makes the MVP a frictionless reusable asset; protect it.

Key Takeaways

  • Deployment, not agent quality, is now the bottleneck — and that is what this book addresses.
  • The MVP is a reusable asset built once around RC-E2E-03 and engineered to generalize across all 38 cataloged services.
  • Three operational truths underpin every later chapter: three layers each with their own lifecycle; same image across two substrates; Independence is structural.
  • Fourth design goal: the developer's inner loop runs at zero Azure cost — laptop tier components are all free and self-hostable.
Chapter 1

The Three-Layer Cake

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Defines the three layers — Platform, Service, Agent — and what belongs in each.
  • Maps each layer to its own container registry, its own Bicep module set, and its own version line.
  • Shows how the three layers compose for a single tenant running RC-E2E-03 today and many services tomorrow.
60-second read
  • Three layers · three lifecycles: Platform (per tenant · quarterly) · Service (per envelope · per upgrade window) · Agent (continuous · per merge to main).
  • Two container registries: Deloitte authoring registry for build & sign · per-tenant client registry for runtime pull. Images cross by mirror + cosign verify.
  • Service Bicep references agent images by SHA digest · Layer 1 outputs feed Layer 2 parameters. The cake works when the layers stay separated.

1.1 The Layers

APEX deploys in three layers. Read top-down they look like a stack; read bottom-up they look like a cake. Either visual works as long as you keep them separate.

Layer 3 — Agent
The reusable container images that implement individual agents (The Analyst, The Demand Checker, The Finance Lead, The Operations Lead, The Briefer). Built and signed in the Deloitte authoring registry; mirrored to a client registry; pulled by SHA digest at runtime. Versioned independently. Reused across many Services.
apex-acr.azurecr.io/agent-the-analyst:1.4.0 → mirrored → client-tenant registry
Layer 2 — Service
A productized envelope that composes one or more Agents over the Platform — KPI envelope, scope, persona, audit-row pattern, two-contract commercial. RC-E2E-03 is one Service. There are 38 in the catalog. Each Service has its own Bicep module set and its own version line; many Services can co-exist in the same tenant on the same Platform.
rc-e2e-03 v1.2.0 :: composes [the-analyst, the-demand-checker, the-finance-lead, the-operations-lead, the-briefer]
Layer 1 — APEX Platform
The shared substrate, deployed once per tenant: Container Apps Environment, Premium Azure Container Registry with Notary trust, four user-assigned managed identities, Premium HSM Key Vault with CMK, Log Analytics + App Insights, Networking, Purview catalog hooks. Every Service in the tenant runs on this substrate.
apex-platform v0.1.0 :: per-tenant deployment of foundation Bicep

The single most important sentence in this chapter: each layer has its own version, its own container registry, and its own deployment cadence. If your team's CI pipeline rebuilds the Platform every time an agent prompt changes, you have collapsed Layers 1 and 3 and you will pay for it on the third tenant onboarding.

Layer 3 — Agent images Layer 2 — Service envelope Layer 1 — APEX Platform

1.2 Why Three and Not Two

It is tempting to merge Service and Platform into one "deployment unit." That works at exactly one Service per tenant. The moment a tenant runs both RC-E2E-03 (Pricing & Revenue) and RC-E2E-04 (Loyalty churn), they share Platform but not Service. The Container Apps Environment, container registry, Key Vault, and Log Analytics workspace are common; the agents, the audit-row schema specialization, and the commercial envelope are per-Service.

It is equally tempting to merge Agent into Service — to think of "the RC-E2E-03 agent stack" as the unit. That breaks reuse. The Analyst appears in RC-E2E-03 (severity-rank cold-chain excursions), in HLS-E2E-04 (severity-rank prior-auth queues), and in TH-AIR-02 (severity-rank IROPS re-accommodations). One container image, three Services, three different fixtures and prompts. If the image is owned by the Service, you build three. If it is owned by Layer 3, you build one and parameterize.

Best Practice

When you draw a deployment diagram, draw three columns labeled Platform, Service, Agent and place every component in exactly one column. If a component is in two columns, you have a layer-violation; refactor before you write Bicep.

1.3 The Three Layers, Concretely

1.3.1 Layer 1 — APEX Platform

Stood up once per tenant. The Sprint 1 Bicep modules in MVP/agentic-merch/bicep/modules/ are the canonical implementation:

BICEP — modules
acr.bicep            // Premium container registry · Notary content trust · runtime AcrPull role
identity.bicep       // 4 user-assigned MIs (runtime, mcp, ingest, pii-unlock)
keyvault.bicep       // Premium HSM · CMK key (RSA-HSM 2048) · runtime + MCP role assignments
observability.bicep  // Log Analytics workspace + App Insights bound by WorkspaceResourceId
agents.bicep         // Container Apps environment (Consumption profile · KEDA)

main.bicep imports those five modules and parameterizes location (default centralus) and deploymentSuffix (default lab01). Outputs are runtimeIdentityId, keyvaultUri, acrLoginServer, and containerAppsEnvId — these are the inputs every Service consumes.

1.3.2 Layer 2 — Service

A Service is a Bicep module set that composes Container Apps onto the Platform's Container Apps Environment, plus per-Service configuration, secrets, and audit-row schema. RC-E2E-03 in Sprint 1 contributes the first Container App definition (agents.bicep includes The Analyst); Sprints 2–5 add the four remaining agents to the same Service. Sprint 6 packages all five into the Lab v0.1 deployment.

By Sprint 6 the Service-level Bicep parameter list looks like this:

BICEP — service params (rc-e2e-03)
param serviceCode string = 'rc-e2e-03'
param serviceVersion string = '1.0.0'
param containerAppsEnvId string  // from Platform output
param runtimeIdentityId string   // from Platform output
param acrLoginServer string      // from Platform output
param keyvaultUri string         // from Platform output
param agentImages object = {
  'the-analyst':         'agent-the-analyst@sha256:...'
  'the-demand-checker':  'agent-the-demand-checker@sha256:...'
  'the-finance-lead':    'agent-the-finance-lead@sha256:...'
  'the-operations-lead': 'agent-the-operations-lead@sha256:...'
  'the-briefer':         'agent-the-briefer@sha256:...'
}

Note that agent images are referenced by SHA digest, not by tag. This is a deliberate Layer-3-to-Layer-2 binding: a Service deployment locks to immutable image content, so a Layer 3 retag cannot silently change Service behavior. Chapter 12 explains the SemVer line for each layer and the compatibility matrix that pins them together.

1.3.3 Layer 3 — Agent

An Agent is a container image. It has a /run endpoint and a /healthz endpoint, both built by build_agent_app(runner=..., agent_id=...) in agentic_merch.base. The runner is the Service-specific behavior; the framework is shared.

PYTHON — agent image entry point
from agentic_merch.base import build_agent_app
from agentic_merch.the_analyst.runner import AnalystRunner

app = build_agent_app(runner=AnalystRunner(), agent_id="the-analyst")

The same image runs unchanged on Docker Desktop (via docker-compose.yml) and on Azure Container Apps (via the Bicep Container App resource). Substrate-awareness is environment-variable-driven and is the entire subject of Chapter 2.

1.4 Two Container Registries, One Path

Layer 3 images flow through two Azure Container Registry instances (Microsoft's Microsoft.ContainerRegistry/registries resource — see the note in How to Use This Book for the abbreviation collision):

  1. Deloitte authoring registry — apex-acr.azurecr.io, owned by the Deloitte engineering team. Builds, scans, and signs all agent images. Premium tier with Notary content trust and cosign verification on push. Source of truth for Layer 3 versions.
  2. Client tenant registry — per client Azure tenant, deployed by Layer 1's acr.bicep. Geo-replicated; receives images mirrored from the Deloitte authoring registry by the client's CI subscription. Cosign signature is verified on pull; an unsigned image cannot start.

The Service's Bicep references the client container registry. The Service never reaches into the Deloitte authoring registry at runtime; that boundary is one of the structural enforcements of the two-contract commercial model. Chapter 11 walks the promotion pipeline.

Warning

Do not let a delivery team pull agent images directly from the Deloitte authoring registry into a client tenant. It works on day one and breaks every audit on day ninety. The mirror is not a convenience layer; it is the boundary between Deloitte-built artifacts and client-deployed runtime.

Deloitte authoring (synthetic fixtures only) Client Azure tenant (per client) Mirror crossing (the only path) Forbidden — direct runtime access

1.5 The Layer Map

ConcernLayer 1 — PlatformLayer 2 — ServiceLayer 3 — Agent
Deployment unitBicep main + 5 modulesBicep service module + Container AppsContainer image (one per agent)
CadenceQuarterly · per tenantPer Service onboarding · per upgradeContinuous · per merge to main
Versioningapex-platform vX.Y.Zrc-e2e-03 vX.Y.Z (etc.)agent-the-analyst vX.Y.Z (etc.)
OwnsACA Env · container registry · Key Vault · MIs · Log AnalyticsContainer Apps · per-Service config · audit schema · KPI envelopeImage · prompts · MCP tool bindings · runner code
Owned byPlatform engineering (Deloitte) → handed to client opsService owner (Deloitte delivery)Agent maintainer (Deloitte engineering)
Container registry locationPer-tenant container registry (Layer 1 outputs login server)References Layer 1 container registryBuilt in Deloitte authoring registry · mirrored to Layer 1 container registry
Image referencesn/a (does not host images)By SHA digestTagged + signed at build; consumed by digest
Try It Out

Open MVP/agentic-merch/bicep/main.bicep and look at what the file imports versus what it parameterizes. The imports are Layer 1. The parameters that flow into Service modules are Layer 1 outputs. The image-digest map is Layer 3. If you can read main.bicep and identify which line belongs to which layer, you understand the cake.

Key Takeaways

  • APEX deploys in three layers — Platform (per tenant), Service (per productized envelope), Agent (per container image) — each with its own version, container registry location, and cadence.
  • Two container registries: a Deloitte authoring registry for build & sign, a per-tenant client container registry for runtime pull. Images cross the boundary by mirror + cosign verify.
  • Service modules consume Platform outputs and reference Agent images by SHA digest, locking each Service deployment to immutable image content.

Deployment Drills

  1. Take the Sprint 1 component list from MVP/docs/deployment.md §13.5 (the ~30-component Lab v0.1 minimum) and assign every component to exactly one of the three layers.
  2. Identify the layer of each: a new agent prompt; a new HSM key rotation; a new audit-row column; a new Container Apps revision; a new tenant.
  3. Write the SemVer of your last MVP merge for each layer it affects. If a single merge bumped versions in all three layers, audit whether the change really required all three.
Chapter 2

Substrate-Aware Architecture

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Explains what "same image, two substrates" means for laptop and Azure deployments.
  • Catalogues the four substrate tiers — laptop, DEV, STAGE, PROD — and what changes between them.
  • Establishes the W1→W2→W3 commitment: the component graph never redraws, only the substrate extends.
60-second read
  • Same container image bytes run on Docker Desktop and Azure Container Apps; substrate awareness lives at the edges (env vars · Bicep params), not in agent code.
  • Four substrate tiers — laptop · DEV (Lab v0.1) · STAGE · PROD — are strict supersets, not divergent forks. Move up; never branch sideways.
  • Configuration source · secret resolution · identity change between substrates; image · HTTP contract · LEDGER schema · audit-row attribution do not.
  • Orchestration: n8n on laptop · Azure Logic Apps Hybrid on Microsoft Platform. Workflow parity enforced by CI on intent.md + n8n.json + logicapp.json.

2.1 The Promise: Same Image, Two Substrates

The container image that The Analyst ships in is the same image that runs on a delivery lead's Docker Desktop and the same image that runs on Azure Container Apps in the Lab. That promise — same image, two substrates — has two consequences.

  1. Every difference between substrates lives at the edges: environment variables consumed by the agent, secrets resolved at startup, ports bound by the substrate orchestrator. Inside the agent code, there are no if substrate == "azure" branches.
  2. The substrate orchestration layer — docker-compose.yml on a laptop, Bicep on Azure — is the only thing that changes when you move. The Bicep treats the image's environment as a translation target for what compose already provided.

If you find substrate logic inside the agent runner code, that is a layer violation. Push it out to the entry-point or to the Bicep / compose layer.

Laptop · free OSS Microsoft platform · paid Stubs (laptop only)

2.2 The Four Substrate Tiers

The MVP recognizes four substrate tiers, documented in MVP/docs/deployment.md:

TierSubstrateComponent countUsed for
LaptopDocker Desktop · docker-compose~12 components (compose + Postgres + 5 agents + harness + topology UX)Solo + Claude pair development; demo on a 30-second laptop spin-up
DEV (Lab v0.1)Azure Container Apps + Premium container registry + Premium HSM Key Vault~30 components (deployment.md §13.5)Multi-user shared environment; first dual-substrate validation
STAGEAzure Container Apps · client-grade scale-out · Purview integration~50 componentsPre-production with synthetic data and full-scale workloads
PRODPer-tenant Azure deployment · client SOR connectors · LEDGER WORM at scale~75 componentsLive client deployments on client Azure tenants

The point of the four-tier ladder is that each tier is a strict superset of the one below. Components added at DEV are not subtracted at STAGE; they are joined by additional components (Purview catalog hooks, more aggressive observability, real SOR connectors). This is the W1→W2→W3 commitment expressed at deployment: the component graph extends but does not redraw.

Independence — Substrate Tiers

The Lab tier (DEV) lives in a Deloitte-administered Azure subscription with synthetic fixtures only. STAGE and PROD live in client subscriptions; Deloitte deploys into client tenants under the Microsoft-platform contract for that client. Lab artifacts never see client data. Client artifacts never run in the Deloitte authoring tenant. This separation is structural, not operational; it is enforced by the container registry boundary in Chapter 1.

2.3 What Changes Between Substrates

Three things change as you move from laptop to Azure:

2.3.1 Configuration source

On a laptop, configuration comes from .env files referenced by docker-compose.yml. On Azure, configuration comes from Container App env vars and Key Vault references resolved at startup.

YAML — laptop
# MVP/docker-compose.yml
services:
  agent-the-analyst:
    image: agent-the-analyst:dev
    environment:
      AGENT_ID: the-analyst
      LEDGER_BACKEND: postgres
      LEDGER_DSN: postgres://harness:harness@postgres:5432/harness
      MCP_ENDPOINT: http://harness:8080/mcp
BICEP — Azure
// agents.bicep — Container App env block (excerpt)
env: [
  { name: 'AGENT_ID', value: 'the-analyst' }
  { name: 'LEDGER_BACKEND', value: 'postgres' }
  { name: 'LEDGER_DSN', secretRef: 'ledger-dsn' }
  { name: 'MCP_ENDPOINT', value: mcpEndpoint }
  { name: 'KEYVAULT_URI', value: keyvaultUri }
]

The image consumes the same env-var names. The substrate decides where those names get their values.

2.3.2 Secret resolution

On laptop, secrets are inline strings in .env (acceptable for synthetic fixtures only). On Azure, secrets resolve through Key Vault references attached to the runtime managed identity. The agent code does not know which it is using; it asks the substrate for the value of LEDGER_DSN and gets a string.

2.3.3 Networking and identity

On laptop, the compose network is flat; everything reaches everything by service name. On Azure, the Container Apps Environment provides DNS scoped to the env, ingress is internal-only by default, and any cross-Service traffic flows through the Environment's HTTP routing. Identity moves from "I trust the laptop user" to "I trust the user-assigned managed identity attached to the Container App."

2.4 What Does Not Change

The list of things that do not change is short and load-bearing:

  • The container image bytes. Same SHA on laptop and Azure.
  • The agent's HTTP contract. POST /run with the canonical request body produces the same response shape against the same fixture, on either substrate.
  • The LEDGER row schema. _hash and _prev_hash columns are written identically on laptop Postgres and on Azure Postgres-flavored backends; the chain verifies across substrates.
  • The audit-row attribution. The same cost_per_decision and safety_verdict fields are populated.

This invariance is what makes the test harness portable. The same UC catalog runs against laptop or Azure substrate by changing the harness's target endpoint. The harness does not care; the agent does not care; only the Bicep / compose orchestration knows.

Best Practice

When you add a new env var to an agent, add it to three places in the same merge: the agent's runner that reads it; docker-compose.yml; and the Bicep env: block. If you cannot do all three in one PR, the env var's substrate contract is not yet stable.

2.5 The W1→W2→W3 Commitment, Operationally

The Sellers Guide describes Wave 1, 2, and 3 commercially. Operationally, the commitment is simpler: moving up a wave extends the component graph; it never redraws it. A W2 pilot adds DAG orchestration, agents 1–3, and HITL gates on top of W1's bronze/silver/canonical pipeline; a W3 scale-and-fuse extends with enterprise-scale fuses and a feedback loop. The same applies to substrates: STAGE adds components to DEV; PROD adds components to STAGE.

The practical consequence: when a delivery team asks "do we need to rebuild the Bicep when we move from Lab to client tenant?", the answer is "no — you parameterize the Bicep by tenant and substrate suffix, and the same modules deploy with different inputs." Chapter 13 walks the day-zero onboarding that makes this concrete.

2.6 The Orchestration Substrate: n8n on Laptop, Azure Logic Apps on Microsoft Platform

Architectural shift — substrate is now trigger + entry point, not multi-agent composer

Earlier APEX-M iterations modeled n8n / Logic Apps Hybrid as the multi-agent composer — parallel branches per agent, IF nodes routing on agent output, merge nodes aggregating. Since Microsoft Agent Framework GA, that work moved into Agent Framework's AgentWorkflowBuilder patterns (Sequential · Concurrent · Handoff · Group Chat · Magentic) inside the agent fleet. The substrate retains a narrower role: trigger receiver · authentication · single HTTP POST to the agent fleet · post-response routing · substrate-level LEDGER row. Five nodes per Service · not fifteen.

This deployment chapter still describes both substrates (n8n laptop · Logic Apps Hybrid cloud) — their operational deployment mechanics are unchanged. What changed is what they do. See Services Guide §14.4 (substrate calls into Agent Framework), §14.6.12a (migration table from old pattern to new), and §14.5.8 (the Loader that builds the agent-side workflow).

Agent images are reused across substrates (§2.1); orchestration follows the same parity rule with two concrete substrates. Each substrate's job in the current architecture is to receive a trigger event, authenticate the caller, post once to the agent fleet, and route the agent fleet's response to its operator destination (Microsoft Teams adaptive card · downstream non-agent integration · Microsoft.Storage / Microsoft.DocumentDB write). Two substrates carry that responsibility:

Substrate tierOrchestratorHostingCost posture
Laptopn8n (open-source)Container in docker-compose.yml alongside agents and Postgres$0 — Sustainable Use License covers self-hosted internal use
DEV (Lab v0.1)Azure Logic AppsLogic Apps Hybrid on the Layer 1 Microsoft.App/managedEnvironmentsPay-per-execution; no new hosting plane required
STAGE / PRODAzure Logic AppsLogic Apps Hybrid (default), or Logic Apps Standard on Microsoft.Web/sites (kind=workflowapp) when VNet integration outside Container Apps is requiredPer-tenant; cost attributed via apex:service-code tag
Best Practice — Use Logic Apps Hybrid as the Azure default

Logic Apps Hybrid runs the Logic Apps Standard runtime on top of Microsoft.App/containerApps. The Layer 1 Microsoft.App/managedEnvironments already exists; deploying the Hybrid host into it amortizes the orchestrator across the same compute plane as the agents. Reach for Logic Apps Standard on Microsoft.Web/sites only when you need workflow features Hybrid doesn't yet expose, or when VNet integration must extend outside the Container Apps Environment.

2.6.1 Why n8n on the laptop

n8n was chosen for laptop dev for four reasons:

  • Free and self-hostable. Preserves the §0.5 zero-cost dev guarantee. The n8n Docker image runs in compose with one volume for workflow JSON.
  • Visual node graph. Matches the n8n-style topology UX we use elsewhere in the MVP — the developer sees the same orchestration shape they will deploy to Logic Apps.
  • HTTP-first. n8n's HTTP node calls Container /run endpoints over the docker-compose network with no auth ceremony — exactly mirroring how Logic Apps will call Container Apps internal ingress on Azure.
  • Workflow JSON portability. n8n exports workflows as JSON; conversion to a Logic Apps workflow definition is a translation, not a rewrite. §2.6.3 covers the parity rule.

2.6.2 Why Azure Logic Apps on the Microsoft platform

On Azure, the orchestrator is Microsoft-native because the rest of Layer 1 is:

  • Managed identity binding. A Logic App authenticates as the Layer 1 mi-apex-merch-runtime user-assigned managed identity — no API keys, no client secrets.
  • Microsoft.KeyVault native connector. Secrets resolve through the Logic Apps Key Vault connector against the Layer 1 vault.
  • Microsoft Sentinel correlation. Logic App run history lands in the same Microsoft.OperationalInsights/workspaces as the agent traces; Sentinel rules correlate orchestrator decisions with agent behavior.
  • Microsoft Entra ID conditional access. The same conditional-access policy that protects the agents protects the Logic App.
  • Microsoft Cost Management attribution. Logic App cost flows under the same apex:service-code tag as the agents it orchestrates.

Azure Logic Apps Hybrid declares as a Microsoft.App/containerApps resource with kind: 'workflowapp' against the existing Container Apps Environment. The workflow definitions sit in workflow.json files mounted via Microsoft.Storage; the Service Bicep references them by path.

Note — two Logic Apps Hybrid hosting fabrics per Microsoft Learn

Per Microsoft Learn, "Logic Apps Hybrid" covers two distinct deployment fabrics. APEX-M defaults to the first; the second is available for clients whose platform investment already includes Arc.

  1. Logic Apps Standard on Container Apps (Microsoft.App/containerApps · kind: 'workflowapp') — APEX-M default. Amortizes onto the same Layer 1 Container Apps Environment as the agents; no new hosting plane required. This is the snippet above.
  2. Logic Apps Hybrid on Azure Arc-enabled Kubernetes (Microsoft.Web/kubeEnvironments backed by an Arc-enabled AKS or conformant Kubernetes cluster, requires a SQL Server runtime state store). Used when the engagement already operates an Arc-enabled K8s footprint (often for hybrid-cloud or on-prem proximity requirements). The workflow authoring surface is identical; the hosting fabric is the difference.
BICEP — Logic Apps Hybrid (excerpt)
resource orchestrator 'Microsoft.App/containerApps@2024-03-01' = {
  name: 'logic-${serviceCode}-orchestrator-${deploymentSuffix}'
  location: location
  kind: 'workflowapp'
  identity: {
    type: 'UserAssigned'
    userAssignedIdentities: { '${runtimeIdentityId}': {} }
  }
  properties: {
    managedEnvironmentId: containerAppsEnvId
    workloadProfileName: 'Consumption'
    configuration: {
      registries: [ { server: acrLoginServer, identity: runtimeIdentityId } ]
      secrets: [
        { name: 'workflow-storage-connection', keyVaultUrl: '${keyvaultUri}secrets/${serviceCode}-workflow-storage', identity: runtimeIdentityId }
      ]
      ingress: { external: false, targetPort: 80, transport: 'http' }
    }
    template: {
      containers: [{
        name: 'logicapps'
        image: 'mcr.microsoft.com/azurelogicapps/logicapps-hybrid:latest'
        env: [
          { name: 'AzureWebJobsStorage', secretRef: 'workflow-storage-connection' }
          { name: 'APP_KIND',           value: 'workflowApp' }
          { name: 'SERVICE_CODE',       value: serviceCode }
          { name: 'AGENT_ANALYST_URL',  value: 'http://app-${serviceCode}-the-analyst-${deploymentSuffix}/run' }
          { name: 'AGENT_BRIEFER_URL',  value: 'http://app-${serviceCode}-the-briefer-${deploymentSuffix}/run' }
        ]
        resources: { cpu: 1, memory: '2Gi' }
      }]
      scale: { minReplicas: 1, maxReplicas: 3 }
    }
  }
}

2.6.3 Workflow Parity — One Author, Two Substrates

The parity contract: a Service author maintains one conceptual workflow per orchestrated flow, and the laptop and Azure substrates each render their substrate-specific JSON. The MVP repository keeps both:

FILE LAYOUT — workflows directory
MVP/agentic-merch/services/rc-e2e-03/workflows/
  excursion-triage/
    workflow.intent.md      # Plain-English description of the orchestration
    workflow.n8n.json       # n8n export — laptop substrate
    workflow.logicapp.json  # Azure Logic Apps Standard workflow definition
  markdown-optimization/
    workflow.intent.md
    workflow.n8n.json
    workflow.logicapp.json

Both JSON files implement the same intent. CI verifies that the two stay in sync: the same set of named steps, the same agents called, the same branch conditions. A drift between them fails the build before either substrate sees it.

The workflow primitives that translate cleanly between n8n and Azure Logic Apps:

Patternn8n node typeAzure Logic Apps action
Event trigger (webhook)WebhookHTTP Request (built-in trigger)
Schedule triggerSchedule TriggerRecurrence trigger
Call agent /runHTTP RequestHTTP action
Branch on resultIF / SwitchCondition / Switch
Parallel fan-outSplit In Batches → parallel branchesParallel branch / ForEach
HITL approvalWait + Webhook resumeApproval connector / Teams Adaptive Card
LEDGER writeHTTP Request → harness ledger endpointHTTP action → harness ledger endpoint, or Cosmos DB / SQL action direct
Warning — Don't reach for n8n features that have no Logic Apps equivalent

n8n has community nodes that don't map cleanly to Logic Apps. If a workflow uses one, the parity contract breaks and the laptop substrate behavior diverges from production. Restrict laptop workflows to the primitives in the table above; if you need richer behavior, push it into an agent (which deploys identically across substrates) rather than into the orchestrator.

2.6.4 Independence and Orchestration

n8n is laptop-only. It does not deploy to client tenants. The Azure-side orchestrator is always Azure Logic Apps so that the tenant deployment is unambiguously Microsoft-native — Logic Apps cost burns under the client's Microsoft contract, runs under the client's Microsoft Entra ID conditional access, and audits under the client's Microsoft Sentinel rules. Running n8n in a client tenant would put a non-Microsoft orchestrator on the critical path of audited decisions and is out of scope for the two-contract model in Chapter 3.

Key Takeaways

  • Same container image runs on Docker Desktop and Azure Container Apps; substrate awareness lives at the edges, not in the agent code.
  • Four substrate tiers — laptop, DEV (Lab v0.1), STAGE, PROD — are strict supersets, not divergent forks.
  • Configuration source, secret resolution, and identity change between substrates; the image, the HTTP contract, the LEDGER schema, and the audit-row attribution do not.
  • W1→W2→W3 extends the component graph; it never redraws it. The same commitment applies to substrate progression.
  • Orchestration substrate: n8n on laptop, Azure Logic Apps on Azure. Logic Apps Hybrid on the existing Microsoft.App/managedEnvironments is the Azure default. Workflow parity is enforced by maintaining substrate-specific JSON for one shared intent.

Deployment Drills

  1. Pick one agent (start with The Analyst). List every env var it reads. For each, mark whether it is provided by compose, by Bicep, by Key Vault reference, or by Service-level config.
  2. Run docker-compose up on your laptop, hit POST /run on each agent, save the response. Then run the same fixtures against the Lab Container App. Diff the responses. There should be none.
  3. Open MVP/docs/deployment.md §13.5. Identify which components in the Lab v0.1 list are added versus configured compared to the laptop tier.
Chapter 3

Independence Posture for Deployment

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Translates Independence from a language guideline into a deployment-architecture constraint.
  • Walks the two-contract model and where it shows up in Bicep, container registry, and ECIF flows.
  • Lists the four phrases that must not appear in any deployment artifact and the language to use instead.
60-second read
  • Independence is enforced architecturally — by the container-registry boundary, the subscription boundary, and the ECIF flow path — not just stylistically.
  • Two contracts: client ↔ Microsoft for the platform · client ↔ Deloitte for Service-delivery engagement. ECIF flows direct from Microsoft.
  • Use "DMTSP" · "Microsoft platform capabilities" · "Microsoft-native deployment." Never partner · alliance · strategic alliance in any artifact that survives the engagement.

3.1 The Two-Contract Model

Independence is the rule that Deloitte's audit relationships with certain clients constrain the way Deloitte and Microsoft can co-sell and co-deliver to those clients. The operational consequence — the only one a deployment architect needs to internalize — is the two-contract model:

  • Contract 1. Client ↔ Microsoft, for the platform. Azure subscription, container registry consumption, Container Apps, Key Vault HSM. ECIF (if any) flows under this contract, direct from Microsoft to the client.
  • Contract 2. Client ↔ Deloitte, for services. Agent images, Service envelope, runbook delivery, support. Paid for by client out of separately negotiated SOWs.

The two contracts must not become one. ECIF must not flow through Deloitte. Co-marketing must not present Deloitte as a Microsoft "partner" or "alliance." This chapter covers what that looks like in Bicep, container registry, and runbooks.

3.2 Language: What to Say, What Not to Say

UseDo not use
Deloitte's Microsoft practiceDeloitte–Microsoft alliance
DMTSP (Deloitte Microsoft Technology & Services Practice)Strategic alliance
Microsoft platform capabilitiesDeloitte–Microsoft partner solution
Microsoft-native deploymentJoint go-to-market offering

These distinctions matter in deployment artifacts because runbooks, README files, and Bicep parameter descriptions outlive the person who wrote them. A README that says "Deployed in partnership with Microsoft" will be quoted back at a procurement review three years later.

Warning

Search every .md and .bicep file in your delivery repo for the substrings partner, alliance, and strategic alliance before any artifact leaves Deloitte. CI hooks for this check are described in Appendix B.

3.3 Architectural Enforcement of the Two Contracts

Independence is not enforced by intent; it is enforced by where artifacts live and whose subscription pays for what.

3.3.1 Container registry boundary

The Deloitte authoring registry is in a Deloitte subscription. The client tenant registry is in the client subscription. Image bytes flow Deloitte → client by mirror + cosign verify. Client deployments pull only from the client registry. There is no path at runtime from a client Container App to the Deloitte authoring registry.

3.3.2 Subscription boundary

Layer 1 Bicep deploys into a client subscription. The Deloitte authoring tenant only ever holds reference deployments (Lab v0.1) with synthetic fixtures. Lab tenants and client tenants share zero data plane; they share only the agent image bytes that flow through the container registry boundary.

3.3.3 ECIF flow

If ECIF (Microsoft's Enterprise Customer Investment Funds) is in play, it flows directly from Microsoft to the client. Deloitte does not invoice the client for Microsoft platform consumption; Microsoft does. Deloitte SOWs are for services only. Bicep has no role in ECIF, but ECIF affects which subscription owns which resource — and that affects which managed identity owns which artifact.

Independence — Bicep parameter naming

When parameterizing tenant-specific Bicep, use names that reflect the boundary: clientTenantId, clientSubscriptionId, clientResourceGroup. Do not name parameters deloittePartnerTenant or jointDeploymentSub. The parameter names are part of the artifact's permanent record.

3.4 What This Means for Day-Zero Onboarding

The deployment motion in Chapter 13 begins with the client provisioning the Azure subscription and granting Deloitte's deployment principal a scoped role to deploy Bicep. The Deloitte principal does not own the subscription; it has a delegated permission set that lets it run az deployment sub create against the client subscription for the duration of the engagement. That is the Independence-compatible deployment posture.

Concretely, Chapter 13's onboarding checklist starts with five client-side actions before any Deloitte action runs. Appendix B has the full list. If you cannot tick those five client-side boxes before running Bicep, the onboarding is not yet structurally Independence-compatible and must not proceed.

3.5 The Five Things to Remember

  1. Two contracts. Microsoft for platform, Deloitte for services. Always two, never one.
  2. ECIF direct, never through Deloitte. The Bicep does not see ECIF; the SOW does.
  3. Two container registries. Deloitte builds and signs; client pulls verified images.
  4. "DMTSP" / "Microsoft-native deployment" / "Microsoft platform capabilities" — never "partner," "alliance," "strategic alliance."
  5. Lab and client tenants share images, never data.

Key Takeaways

  • Independence is enforced architecturally — by the container registry boundary, the subscription boundary, and the ECIF flow path — not just stylistically.
  • Two contracts: Microsoft for platform, Deloitte for services. ECIF flows direct.
  • Use "DMTSP," "Microsoft platform capabilities," "Microsoft-native deployment." Never "partner," "alliance," or "strategic alliance" in any artifact that survives the engagement.

Deployment Drills

  1. Run a regex search across your delivery repo for the four prohibited phrases. Land any clean-up commits before further work.
  2. Walk a deployment runbook and mark every step as "Deloitte-action" or "Client-action." If any step crosses the boundary in a way that a regulator could not reconstruct from the document alone, rewrite it.
  3. Confirm that your Bicep parameter names do not encode the relationship — search for partner, alliance, joint in the parameter blocks.
Part II

Layer 1 — APEX Platform

Day 1. Priya runs azd provision against a blank Azure subscription. Two hours later, the platform stands. A new resource group holds the Microsoft.ContainerRegistry where signed images will live; a Microsoft.App managed environment is ready to host both the agent fleet and the Logic Apps Hybrid orchestrator; four user-assigned managed identities (runtime · MCP · ingest · pii-unlock) have been provisioned and assigned to Microsoft Entra ID; a Microsoft.KeyVault Premium with HSM-backed CMK is taking secrets; a Microsoft.OperationalInsights workspace is collecting logs; Microsoft Defender for Cloud is watching for posture drift. No Service is deployed yet. No agent is running. But the floor is solid.

From this part forward, every component is named by its Microsoft resource type and API version. We don't say "use a key vault" — we say Microsoft.KeyVault/vaults@2024-11-01 because the Bicep does. The Deployment Guide is specified at the version a delivery engineer would copy-paste into their template; anything more abstract drifts from what Priya actually types into VS Code.

Six chapters in this part build Layer 1. Chapter 4 is the anatomy — the resource composition Priya provisions on Day 1. Chapter 5 goes deep on identity, secrets, and audit trust — the four UAMIs, the Key Vault topology, the LEDGER anchor. Chapter 5A covers deploying Microsoft Entra ID and Purview (because Layer 1 isn't just compute; it's identity and governance too). Chapter 6 walks network and observability — VNets, private endpoints, Log Analytics. Chapter 6A covers deploying Microsoft Fabric (the data plane). Chapter 6B closes with Sentinel and Defender — the security operations companion. By the end of Part II, Priya's tenant has every Microsoft service the eventual Service will need · ready and waiting for Layer 2.
