Across all 38 Services, the agent set is small (the same Analyst / Demand Checker / Finance / Operations / Briefer pattern, plus practice-specific specializations); the data plane and persona model are what change. Chapter 11 shows the reuse pattern across Services.

Note — Catalog versus chains

The Featured Chains sheet of APEX-Scenario-Chains.xlsx ties 36 of the 38 services to a flagship scenario with full W1/W2/W3 wave detail. The two services without a featured chain (RC-E2E-06 Workforce Ops, RC-E2E-08 Marketing & Growth, and ER-OG-02 Asset Maintenance — the count varies as featured chains are added) still have catalog entries with brief + KPI. Appendix E lists every Service whether or not it has a featured chain.

7.4 The Service Module — What Sits at Layer 2

A Layer 2 deployment, for any one of the 38 Services, is a Bicep module that contains:

  • One Microsoft.App/containerApps@2024-03-01 per agent the Service composes (typically 4–6).
  • One Microsoft.Storage/storageAccounts@2023-05-01 for the Service's bronze landing (or pointer to a tenant-shared Microsoft Fabric Lakehouse).
  • Per-Service Microsoft.KeyVault/vaults/secrets entries written into the Layer 1 Key Vault with Service-prefixed names.
  • Service-level Microsoft.Authorization/roleAssignments that grant the Layer 1 runtime managed identity Service-scoped data access (e.g., Storage Blob Data Reader on the Service's silver container).
  • Optionally Microsoft.DocumentDB/databaseAccounts@2024-05-15 or Microsoft.Sql/servers/databases for Service-specific transactional state.

What a Service module does not contain: an container registry, a Key Vault, a managed identity, a Container Apps Environment, a Log Analytics workspace. Those are Layer 1; reaching for them from Layer 2 is a layer violation.

Key Takeaways

  • A Service is the unit of deployment, the unit of commercial envelope, and the unit of audit attribution — 1:1:1, enforced.
  • RC-E2E-03 is the canonical example: 5 agents, 2 scenarios, KPI envelope GM +3.2pp / DoH −28% / Markdown-to-Clear +41% / $142K margin protected.
  • The catalog has 38 Services across 7 practices (RC · HLS · ER · AXLE · TH · TMT · ICE), enumerated in Appendix E.
  • A Service Bicep deploys Microsoft.App/containerApps + Service-scoped data plane atop Layer 1; it never duplicates Layer 1 resources.

Deployment Drills

  1. Open commercial/RC-E2E-03-service-envelope.md. Identify each of the three definitions of "Service" — deployment, commercial, audit — and where the envelope document anchors each.
  2. From Appendix E, pick a Service in a practice you have not worked in. List the agents you would expect it to compose. Compare against the cataloged scenario.
  3. For RC-E2E-03 in your Lab, confirm the 1:1:1: one Bicep module, one envelope file, one service_code='RC-E2E-03' in LEDGER rows.
Chapter 8

Service Composition Atop Platform

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Walks the RC-E2E-03 Service Bicep module end-to-end against the Microsoft.App resource type.
  • Shows how Layer 1 outputs become Layer 2 parameters and how agent images are referenced by SHA digest.
  • Maps the data plane — Microsoft.Storage, Microsoft Fabric, Microsoft.DocumentDB — that a Service composes per scenario.
60-second read
  • Service Bicep parameters consume Layer 1 outputs verbatim and reference agent images by @sha256:... digest.
  • Per-Service data plane: Microsoft.Storage (bronze/silver) · Microsoft.DocumentDB Cosmos DB · Microsoft.Sql or Microsoft.DBforPostgreSQL Flexible Server for LEDGER · Microsoft.Fabric for Gold.
  • KEDA scaling rules vary per agent within a Service — HTTP · Service Bus · cron — declared inside the Microsoft.App/containerApps resource.
  • Workload profile is a per-agent choice (Consumption vs Dedicated D4) inside the Service.

8.1 The Service Module Skeleton

The Layer 2 Bicep for RC-E2E-03 — produced incrementally across Sprints 1–6 in the MVP — has this shape by Sprint 6:

BICEP — services/rc-e2e-03/service.bicep (skeleton)
// Service: RC-E2E-03 Pricing & Revenue Decision Service

// === Layer 1 inputs (consumed verbatim) ===
param containerAppsEnvId      string  // Microsoft.App/managedEnvironments id
param runtimeIdentityId       string  // Microsoft.ManagedIdentity user-assigned id
param mcpIdentityId           string
param acrLoginServer          string  // 'apexacr<suffix>.azurecr.io'
param keyvaultUri             string
param logAnalyticsWorkspaceId string
param appInsightsConnectionString string

// === Service-level config ===
param serviceCode             string = 'RC-E2E-03'
param serviceVersion          string = '1.0.0'
param deploymentSuffix        string

// === Agent image map (referenced by SHA digest, not tag) ===
param agentImages object = {
  'the-analyst':         'agent-the-analyst@sha256:6a4c...'
  'the-demand-checker':  'agent-the-demand-checker@sha256:9b1e...'
  'the-finance-lead':    'agent-the-finance-lead@sha256:c08f...'
  'the-operations-lead': 'agent-the-operations-lead@sha256:42dd...'
  'the-briefer':         'agent-the-briefer@sha256:7e22...'
}

// === Resources ===
resource agents 'Microsoft.App/containerApps@2024-03-01' = [for agentId in items(agentImages): {
  name: 'app-${serviceCode}-${agentId.key}-${deploymentSuffix}'
  location: resourceGroup().location
  identity: {
    type: 'UserAssigned'
    userAssignedIdentities: {
      '${runtimeIdentityId}': {}
      '${mcpIdentityId}': {}
    }
  }
  properties: {
    managedEnvironmentId: containerAppsEnvId
    workloadProfileName: agentId.key == 'the-operations-lead' ? 'D4' : 'Consumption'
    configuration: {
      registries: [
        { server: acrLoginServer, identity: runtimeIdentityId }
      ]
      secrets: [
        { name: 'ledger-dsn', keyVaultUrl: '${keyvaultUri}secrets/${serviceCode}-ledger-dsn', identity: runtimeIdentityId }
      ]
      ingress: { external: false, targetPort: 8000, transport: 'http' }
    }
    template: {
      containers: [{
        name: agentId.key
        image: '${acrLoginServer}/${agentId.value}'
        env: [
          { name: 'AGENT_ID',     value: agentId.key }
          { name: 'SERVICE_CODE', value: serviceCode }
          { name: 'LEDGER_DSN',   secretRef: 'ledger-dsn' }
          { name: 'KEYVAULT_URI', value: keyvaultUri }
          { name: 'APPLICATIONINSIGHTS_CONNECTION_STRING', value: appInsightsConnectionString }
        ]
        probes: [
          { type: 'Liveness',  httpGet: { path: '/healthz', port: 8000 }, initialDelaySeconds: 5, periodSeconds: 10 }
          { type: 'Readiness', httpGet: { path: '/healthz', port: 8000 }, initialDelaySeconds: 2, periodSeconds: 5 }
        ]
        resources: { cpu: 1, memory: '2Gi' }
      }]
      scale: {
        minReplicas: 0
        maxReplicas: 5
        rules: [{
          name: 'http-rule'
          http: { metadata: { concurrentRequests: '10' } }
        }]
      }
    }
  }
}]

Three things to notice. First, every Layer 1 output appears as a parameter — there is no resource lookup, no existing, no cross-deployment side-channel. Second, agent images are referenced by @sha256:... digests, not by tag, locking the deployment to immutable bytes. Third, the workload profile is per-agent: the-operations-lead uses Dedicated D4 because its workload is steady-state high-throughput; the others run on Consumption with scale-to-zero.

8.2 Why SHA-Digest References, Not Tags

Microsoft.ContainerRegistry resolves both image:1.4.0 and image@sha256:6a4c... at pull time. The difference: a tag can be re-pointed to a different image on the registry side without the consumer's awareness, and a Container App revision will pick up the new bytes on its next replica scale-out. A digest cannot. Pinning by digest is the Microsoft-platform-native way to make a Layer 2 deployment record reflect the exact Layer 3 bytes that ran.

The promotion pipeline (Chapter 10 / Appendix D) writes the digest of the signed agent image into the Service Bicep parameter file when the agent is promoted to a Service. The parameter file is checked in. The audit trail of "which Service version ran which agent bytes" is git-replicable.

Warning — Tag drift

If you ever use image:latest in a Service Bicep, your Service deployment is not reproducible. The Container App revision is reproducible (the revision template is locked), but the next scale-out pulls whatever latest currently points to. This is a Microsoft.ContainerRegistry behavior, not a Container Apps bug — it is intentional, and SHA-digest pinning is the documented mitigation.

8.3 The Per-Service Data Plane

Layer 2 also deploys per-Service data plane. For RC-E2E-03, that is:

Microsoft resource typeAPI versionPurpose · RC-E2E-03
Microsoft.Storage/storageAccounts2023-05-01Bronze landing for POS + refrigeration telemetry · ERP + competitive pricing feeds
Microsoft.Storage/storageAccounts/blobServices/containers2023-05-01Per-zone containers (bronze, silver, canonical-merml, canonical-scml)
Microsoft.DocumentDB/databaseAccounts2024-05-15Cosmos DB · per-decision state · session continuity for HITL approvals
Microsoft.Sql/servers/databases2023-05-01-previewAzure SQL Database · LEDGER table (with hash chain) for the Service
Microsoft.Fabric/capacities (per tenant, shared)2023-11-01Fabric Lakehouse for Gold serving — referenced by Service, owned by tenant

The Sprint 1 MVP runs Postgres in docker-compose for the laptop tier. On Azure DEV through PROD, the equivalent is Azure Database for PostgreSQL Flexible Server (Microsoft.DBforPostgreSQL/flexibleServers@2023-12-01-preview) until the LEDGER moves to Azure SQL Database for tighter Microsoft Sentinel integration in STAGE+. The migration is a Layer 2 concern — the agent images do not know which backend they are writing to; they speak SQL.

Note — When to move to Azure SQL

Postgres Flexible Server is fine through DEV and most of STAGE. Azure SQL Database becomes the right answer when the LEDGER's audit-row volume crosses ~100M rows and Microsoft Sentinel correlation rules need T-SQL temporal table semantics. Plan the cutover at the STAGE-to-PROD boundary, not later.

8.4 Per-Service KEDA Scaling Rules

Microsoft.App/containerApps' KEDA-based scaling lets each agent in a Service scale on different signals. RC-E2E-03 uses three different rules:

  • The Analyst — HTTP rule, concurrentRequests=10, min=0 max=5. Bursty: cold-chain excursions are episodic.
  • The Operations Lead — Service Bus queue rule (azure-servicebus KEDA scaler), min=2 max=10. Steady stream of HITL decisions; D4 dedicated profile.
  • The Briefer — Cron rule, scaling to 1 replica every 30 minutes for the digest builds, otherwise scale-to-zero.

KEDA is built into the Microsoft.App/managedEnvironments runtime — there is no separate Bicep resource for the scaler. The rules are declared on each Microsoft.App/containerApps resource and Microsoft's runtime evaluates them.

Key Takeaways

  • Service Bicep parameters consume Layer 1 outputs verbatim and reference agent images by @sha256:... digest.
  • Per-Service data plane: Microsoft.Storage (bronze/silver), Microsoft.DocumentDB Cosmos DB, Microsoft.Sql or Microsoft.DBforPostgreSQL Flexible Server for LEDGER, Microsoft.Fabric for Gold.
  • KEDA scaling rules vary per agent within a Service — HTTP, Service Bus, cron — declared inside the Microsoft.App/containerApps resource.
  • Workload profile is a per-agent choice within the Service (Consumption vs Dedicated D4).

Deployment Drills

  1. Inspect the Sprint 6 Service Bicep parameter file. Confirm every agent image is pinned with @sha256:... and the digests match what's in the per-tenant container registry.
  2. For each agent in your Service, decide its scaling profile (Consumption vs D4) and rule (HTTP / Service Bus / cron). Document the rationale next to the resource.
  3. Find one Service in Appendix E whose data plane would not fit the RC-E2E-03 shape (e.g., HLS-LS-03 trial matching, ER-OG-07 pipeline leak). Sketch the Microsoft resource list it would compose differently.
Chapter 9

Multi-Service per Tenant

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Shows how a single tenant scales from one Service to many on the same Layer 1 Platform.
  • Walks the resource-naming convention that keeps multi-Service deployments unambiguous in Microsoft.Resources.
  • Explains shared-versus-Service-scoped resources and where the boundary sits.
60-second read
  • The Layer 1 Platform amortizes across N Services in the same tenant; the second through fortieth Service onboardings are cheap because Layer 1 is reused.
  • Naming convention is Microsoft.Resources-friendly: apex-*-<suffix> for Layer 1 · app-<service>-<agent>-<suffix> for Layer 2.
  • Microsoft.Resources tags drive Microsoft Cost Management aggregation per Service · feeds the W3 outcome-based commercial envelope.
  • Cross-Service dependencies are agent-level (MCP calls + scoped role assignments), not Bicep-level — the Bicep owners stay decoupled.

9.1 The First Service Is the Hard One

The first Service in a tenant is harder than the second through fortieth. The first Service has to motivate the Layer 1 Bicep deploy, the Day-Zero checklist, the Microsoft Defender enablement, the Microsoft Entra ID conditional-access policies, the Microsoft Sentinel solution. After the first Service lands, every subsequent Service is "another Layer 2 module on top of the same Layer 1."

That asymmetry is by design. A client investing in APEX gets the Platform amortized across N Services. The MVP is built to make N grow easily once the first Service is on the substrate.

9.2 Naming, Tagging, and Microsoft.Resources Hygiene

When a tenant runs RC-E2E-03 alongside RC-E2E-04 and RC-E2E-05, the Microsoft.Resources lookup needs an unambiguous naming convention. The MVP convention:

NAMING — Microsoft resource convention
// Layer 1 — once per tenant
apexacr<suffix>                                  // Microsoft.ContainerRegistry/registries
apexkv<suffix>                                   // Microsoft.KeyVault/vaults
apex-law-<suffix>                                // Microsoft.OperationalInsights/workspaces
apex-ai-<suffix>                                 // Microsoft.Insights/components
apex-env-<suffix>                                // Microsoft.App/managedEnvironments
mi-apex-merch-{runtime|mcp|ingest|pii-unlock}-<suffix>  // Microsoft.ManagedIdentity

// Layer 2 — per Service, per agent
app-<service-code>-<agent-id>-<suffix>            // Microsoft.App/containerApps
st<service-code-flat><suffix>                    // Microsoft.Storage/storageAccounts
cosmos-<service-code>-<suffix>                   // Microsoft.DocumentDB/databaseAccounts
sql-<service-code>-<suffix>                      // Microsoft.Sql/servers

And every resource carries a Microsoft-Resources tag set:

BICEP — tags object
var tags = {
  'apex:layer':         'service' // or 'platform', 'agent-runtime'
  'apex:service-code':  'RC-E2E-03'
  'apex:service-version': '1.0.0'
  'apex:practice':      'RC'
  'apex:domain':        'Pricing & Revenue'
  'apex:tenant-suffix': deploymentSuffix
  'apex:owner':         'deloitte-mvp-engineering'
}

Tagging is Microsoft.Resources-native; Microsoft Cost Management aggregates by tag for per-Service cost attribution, which feeds the W3 outcome-based commercial envelope.

9.3 What's Shared, What's Per-Service

Microsoft resourceShared at tenantPer Service
Microsoft.ContainerRegistry/registries✓ (one per tenant)
Microsoft.App/managedEnvironments✓ (one per tenant)
Microsoft.KeyVault/vaults (Layer 1)✓ (one per tenant)
Microsoft.KeyVault/vaults/secrets✓ (Service-prefixed)
Microsoft.ManagedIdentity (4 identities)✓ (one set per tenant)
Microsoft.OperationalInsights/workspaces✓ (one per tenant)
Microsoft.Insights/components✓ (one per tenant)
Microsoft.App/containerApps✓ (one per agent per Service)
Microsoft.Storage/storageAccounts✓ (one per Service)
Microsoft.DocumentDB / Microsoft.Sql✓ (one per Service)
Microsoft.Fabric/capacities✓ (Lakehouse shared, workspace per Service)
Microsoft Sentinel solution✓ (on Layer 1 workspace)
Microsoft Defender for Cloud / Containers✓ (subscription scope)

One Layer 1, N Layer 2's. The "shared" column does not change between the third Service onboarding and the fortieth; the "per Service" column repeats. That is the entire point of the cake.

9.4 Cross-Service Dependencies

Some scenarios in the catalog cross Service boundaries — for example, the cold-chain excursion scenario in Appendix E references both RC-E2E-03 (Pricing & Revenue) and RC-E2E-09 (Product Tracking, FSMA 204 lot provenance). When two Services compose at runtime, the dependency lives at the agent level, not at the Bicep level:

  • RC-E2E-03's The Analyst calls an MCP tool that queries SCML.Lot data owned by RC-E2E-09.
  • The MCP call traverses the Microsoft.App/managedEnvironments environment routing — both Services are in the same environment, so the call is internal.
  • The runtime managed identity for RC-E2E-03 is granted Storage Blob Data Reader on RC-E2E-09's silver container; the role assignment lives in RC-E2E-09's Bicep, not in RC-E2E-03's.
Best Practice — Read-from-other-Service authorization

When Service A needs to read Service B's data, put the Microsoft.Authorization role assignment in Service B's Bicep (B grants A access). This keeps the access-grant graph readable by walking outward from the data owner, which is what auditors do. The reverse — A's Bicep granting itself access — works mechanically but inverts the audit narrative.

Key Takeaways

  • The Layer 1 Platform amortizes across N Services in the same tenant; the second through fortieth Service onboardings are cheap because Layer 1 is reused.
  • Naming convention is Microsoft.Resources-friendly: apex-*-<suffix> for Layer 1, app-<service>-<agent>-<suffix> for Layer 2.
  • Microsoft-Resources tags drive Microsoft Cost Management aggregation per Service, which feeds the W3 outcome-based commercial envelope.
  • Cross-Service dependencies are agent-level (MCP calls + scoped role assignments), not Bicep-level — the Bicep owners stay decoupled.

Deployment Drills

  1. List the Microsoft resources in your Lab and split them by the §9.3 table. Identify any Layer 1 resources that have been duplicated per Service — those are layer violations.
  2. Run a Microsoft Cost Management query grouped by apex:service-code. If RC-E2E-03 cost is not attributable, audit whether the tag is propagating to all Service-owned resources.
  3. For one cross-Service scenario (e.g., RC-E2E-03 ↔ RC-E2E-09 cold-chain), trace the role-assignment chain. Confirm the grant lives in the data-owner's Bicep.
Part IV

Layer 3 — Agent

Day 31. The Service is wired but its container slots are empty. Priya needs to pull The Pricer, The Demand Checker, The Finance Lead, The Operations Lead, and The Analyst — five published agent images, by SHA digest, from the Deloitte authoring registry, into the client tenant's own Microsoft.ContainerRegistry. The same image bytes serve every Service in the catalog that needs The Pricer; only the runtime configuration changes per Service. Priya doesn't rebuild. She pulls.

An Agent is a Microsoft.ContainerRegistry-hosted container image. It is built once in the Deloitte authoring registry by the agent-authoring team; signed with Microsoft Notation and cosign; the signature attestation is verified at every pull; the SHA digest is what the Service Bicep references. By the time an image arrives in a client tenant, every byte is provably traceable to the source commit and the build pipeline that produced it. This is the discipline that lets one image run unchanged in Sprint 42 of the engagement, in production for two years, and still cleanly upgrade in Wave 3.

Six chapters cover the agent layer. Chapter 10 walks the image pipeline — what the agent author ships, how the image arrives in the client registry. Chapter 11 covers agent reuse — why the same image serves every Service that lists it in its Bicep. Chapter 11.5 is the deployment-time integration with Microsoft Agent Framework — how the Loader binds the image's runtime to the Service's MCP tools and HITL config. Chapter 11.6 deploys the Responsible AI controls (Azure AI Content Safety, eval-gate scripts, model-card publication). Chapter 12 covers versioning and compatibility — the SemVer per layer, the compatibility matrix that pins a Service version to compatible agent ranges. Chapter 12A closes with the networking and observability deep dive that ties everything to Sentinel and Log Analytics.

Chapters in this Part

  • Chapter 10Agent Anatomy and the Image Pipeline
  • Chapter 11Agent Reuse Across Services
  • Chapter 11.5Deploying Microsoft Agent Framework Agents
  • Chapter 11.6Deploying Responsible AI Controls
  • Chapter 12Versioning and Compatibility
  • Chapter 12ANetworking & Observability · Deep Dive
Chapter 10

Agent Anatomy and the Image Pipeline

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Walks the agent base image, the runner contract, and the FastAPI surface every agent exposes.
  • Maps the Microsoft.ContainerRegistry build → sign → scan → mirror pipeline with explicit Microsoft tooling.
  • Anchors trust to Microsoft Notation and cosign with a Microsoft Defender for Containers scan gate.
60-second read
  • Agent images expose a uniform /run + /healthz FastAPI surface — the only Layer 2 ↔ Layer 3 coupling.
  • Pipeline: Microsoft.ContainerRegistry build → Microsoft Defender for Containers scan → Microsoft Notation + cosign sign → notation verify → ACR import mirror → pull-time verify.
  • Trust policy is "strict" with explicit Deloitte authoring trust store; weaker levels void the audit narrative.
  • The mirror across registries is the structural enforcement of the two-contract Independence boundary at Layer 3.

10.1 The Agent Image, From Inside

An agent image is small and uniform. The MVP's Dockerfile.agent-base in MVP/agentic-merch/ produces a base image; per-agent Dockerfiles (e.g., the_analyst/Dockerfile) layer the runner on top.

DOCKERFILE — the_analyst/Dockerfile
FROM apex-acr.azurecr.io/agent-base:1.0.0
COPY src/agentic_merch/the_analyst /app/agentic_merch/the_analyst
ENV AGENT_ID=the-analyst
EXPOSE 8000
CMD ["uvicorn", "agentic_merch.the_analyst:app", "--host", "0.0.0.0", "--port", "8000"]

The runner contract is established by build_agent_app(runner=..., agent_id=...) in agentic_merch.base:

PYTHON — agent FastAPI surface
from fastapi import FastAPI
from agentic_merch.base import build_agent_app
from agentic_merch.the_analyst.runner import AnalystRunner

app: FastAPI = build_agent_app(runner=AnalystRunner(), agent_id="the-analyst")

# Surface contract:
#   POST /run   -> { "request_id", "service_code", "scenario", "inputs": {...} }
#   GET  /healthz -> { "status": "ok", "agent_id": "...", "version": "..." }

This contract is the only coupling between Layer 2 (Service) and Layer 3 (Agent). A Service Bicep references the image; the Container App calls POST /run; the agent writes a LEDGER row. Everything else — prompts, MCP tool catalogs, fixtures, severity-rank weights — lives inside the image.

10.2 The Microsoft.ContainerRegistry Pipeline

An agent image traverses six stages, each a Microsoft platform action:

  1. Build — az acr build against the Deloitte authoring registry (apex-acr.azurecr.io). The build runs inside Microsoft.ContainerRegistry's task service; no laptop docker daemon required.
  2. Scan — Microsoft Defender for Containers auto-scans on push. Vulnerabilities above policy threshold block the next stage.
  3. Sign — Microsoft Notation (notation sign) and cosign (cosign sign) produce two signatures stored as ORAS artifacts alongside the image. Notation is Microsoft-native; cosign is included for cross-tool ecosystem compatibility.
  4. Verify — notation verify confirms the signature matches the trust policy. Unsigned images cannot proceed.
  5. Mirror — image bytes mirror to the client-tenant Microsoft.ContainerRegistry by Microsoft.ContainerRegistry's import API (az acr import). Signatures mirror as ORAS artifacts on the same call.
  6. Pull — Container App revision pulls by SHA digest, with notation verify at pull time. A signature mismatch or trust-policy violation prevents the replica from starting.
SHELL — pipeline invocation (Microsoft tooling)
# 1. Build
az acr build --registry apex-acr \
    --image agent-the-analyst:1.4.0 \
    --file the_analyst/Dockerfile .

# 2. Scan (auto by Microsoft Defender for Containers)
az security assessment show \
    --assessed-resource-id "/subscriptions/.../registries/apex-acr"

# 3. Sign (Microsoft Notation + cosign)
notation sign apex-acr.azurecr.io/agent-the-analyst:1.4.0 \
    --key apex-signing-key
cosign sign apex-acr.azurecr.io/agent-the-analyst:1.4.0

# 4. Verify
notation verify apex-acr.azurecr.io/agent-the-analyst:1.4.0

# 5. Mirror to client-tenant container registry
az acr import \
    --name apexacrlab01 \
    --source apex-acr.azurecr.io/agent-the-analyst:1.4.0

# 6. Pull is implicit (Container App revision triggers it)
Independence — The mirror is the boundary

The mirror in step 5 is the only artifact that crosses from the Deloitte authoring tenant to a client tenant. It carries image bytes + signatures, never source, never secrets, never client data. Audit reconstructions can show, for any client deployment, exactly which image digest was mirrored and when, signed by whom, scanned by which Microsoft Defender policy version. That paper trail is the structural proof of the two-contract model at the Layer 3 boundary.

10.3 The Trust Policy

Microsoft Notation reads its trust policy from a JSON document. A representative APEX trust policy:

JSON — notation trust policy
{
  "version": "1.0",
  "trustPolicies": [
    {
      "name": "apex-strict",
      "registryScopes": [ "apexacrlab01.azurecr.io/agent-*" ],
      "signatureVerification": {
        "level": "strict",
        "override": {}
      },
      "trustStores": [ "ca:apex-deloitte-authoring" ],
      "trustedIdentities": [ "x509.subject:CN=APEX Authoring,O=Deloitte" ]
    }
  ]
}

"Strict" is non-negotiable. Any policy weaker than strict — for example, "permissive" with override allowed — opens a path to running unsigned bytes in a client tenant, which would void the audit narrative.

10.4 Microsoft Defender for Containers Scan Gate

Microsoft Defender for Containers scans every image on push and on pull and emits assessments to Microsoft Defender for Cloud. The pipeline reads the assessment and gates promotion:

SeverityActionRationale
High / CriticalBlock — fail the buildVulnerable bytes do not progress beyond Deloitte authoring registry
MediumBlock by default; document override requiredOverride needs explicit Service-owner signoff with mitigating-control reference
Low / InformationalAllow with loggingTracked, not blocking

Microsoft Defender for Containers also performs runtime threat detection on the running Container App — anomalous network calls, suspicious process trees, container-escape attempts. That is a Layer 1 / Layer 2 concern; the scan gate above is purely Layer 3.

Key Takeaways

  • Agent images expose a uniform /run + /healthz FastAPI surface — the only Layer 2 ↔ Layer 3 coupling.
  • Pipeline: Microsoft.ContainerRegistry build → Microsoft Defender for Containers scan → Microsoft Notation + cosign sign → notation verify → container registry import mirror → pull-time verify.
  • Trust policy is "strict" with an explicit Deloitte authoring trust store; weaker levels void the audit narrative.
  • The mirror across registries is the structural enforcement of the two-contract Independence boundary at Layer 3.

Deployment Drills

  1. Walk the six pipeline stages against your Lab. For each, find the Microsoft tool invocation and the artifact it produces.
  2. Inspect the notation trust policy currently in effect for your client-tenant container registry. Confirm it is set to "strict" and the trustedIdentities list matches the Deloitte authoring CA.
  3. Run az acr repository show-manifests on your authoring container registry for one agent image; identify the ORAS artifacts holding the Notation and cosign signatures.
Chapter 11

Agent Reuse Across Services

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Shows how a single agent image serves multiple Services across multiple practices in the 38-Service catalog.
  • Maps the parameterization model — fixtures, prompts, MCP tool bindings — that lets one image be five different things at runtime.
  • Establishes the rule: image bytes are reused; runtime parameters specialize.
60-second read
  • One Layer 3 image serves many Layer 2 Services; bytes are reused, runtime parameters specialize.
  • Three parameter sources: image-baked defaults · Container App env vars · Microsoft.KeyVault-backed secrets — in increasing scope.
  • Across the 38-Service catalog, ~8–10 reusable agent images plus ~10 practice-specific specializations cover the operational core.
  • Fork an image when logic changes fundamentally; never to change a config value.

11.1 One Image, Many Services

Take The Analyst. Its responsibility is severity-ranking — given a stream of events with attributes, assign a severity score and a recommended action class. The bytes that do this are industry-neutral. The same image can serve:

ServicePracticeWhat The Analyst severity-ranks
RC-E2E-03Retail & ConsumerCold-chain excursion events; markdown candidates by SKU-store
HLS-E2E-04Health & Life SciencesPrior-authorization queue items by clinical urgency
HLS-E2E-02Health & Life SciencesClaims-denial appeals by recovery probability
TH-AIR-02Travel & HospitalityIROPS re-accommodation cases by passenger tier × misconnect risk
ER-PU-04Energy & ResourcesDistribution outages by customer-minutes-impacted
TMT-TEL-NET-02TMTNetwork fault tickets by predictive-degradation score

The image bytes are identical across all six. What differs:

  • The runner-config bundle, mounted via Microsoft.KeyVault secret reference at startup, telling the runner which scoring weights, which fixture schema, which severity-class mapping to use.
  • The MCP tool catalog the runner is allowed to call, scoped per Service via the MCP managed identity's role assignments.
  • The LEDGER service_code column populated at decision time.

11.2 The Parameterization Model

Three parameter sources, in increasing scope:

11.2.1 Image-baked defaults

The image carries a default config (severity threshold, output schema). A demo on docker-compose with no env-var overrides runs against the default — typically the RC-E2E-03 fixture.

11.2.2 Container App env vars

The Service Bicep sets per-Service env vars on the Container App. For HLS-E2E-04 deploying The Analyst:

BICEP — HLS-E2E-04 reuse of The Analyst
env: [
  { name: 'AGENT_ID',     value: 'the-analyst' }
  { name: 'SERVICE_CODE', value: 'HLS-E2E-04' }
  { name: 'CONFIG_BUNDLE', value: 'config-hls-e2e-04-prior-auth' }
  { name: 'FIXTURE_NAMESPACE', value: 'hls/prior-auth' }
  { name: 'SEVERITY_WEIGHTS_KEY', secretRef: 'hls-e2e-04-severity-weights' }
]

11.2.3 Microsoft.KeyVault-backed runtime secrets

Service-specific weights, prompts, and rule-pack references live as Microsoft.KeyVault/vaults/secrets entries in the Layer 1 vault, prefixed by service code. The agent reads them via the runtime managed identity. New Services do not require a new image — they require a new set of Key Vault secrets and a new Service Bicep that references them.

Best Practice — When to fork an image

If two Services need fundamentally different agent logic (not just different weights or prompts) — for example, an Analyst that scores by Markov chain vs an Analyst that scores by gradient-boosted trees — fork the image into agent-the-analyst-tree as a sibling Layer 3 artifact. Do not fork the image to change a config value.

11.3 Reuse Pattern Across the 38-Service Catalog

Looking across the catalog in Appendix E, the reusable agent set is small:

Agent imageFunctionApprox Services served (of 38)
agent-the-analystSeverity-rank events / queue items~25 (every Service with a triage moment)
agent-the-demand-checkerEstimate elasticity / demand response under proposed action~18 (Services with a quantity decision)
agent-the-finance-leadP&L impact & commercial-envelope check~22
agent-the-operations-leadHITL approval composer · operator-friendly action card~30 (any Service with a HITL gate)
agent-the-brieferEnd-of-shift / weekly digest builder~32 (almost every Service produces a digest)
+ practice-specific specializationse.g., AXLE-Connected-Factory anomaly detector1–4 each

Roughly 8–10 agent images cover the operational core of all 38 Services, with another dozen practice-specific specializations for cases the core five do not fit. That is the leverage of treating Layer 3 as its own line: build twelve images well, parameterize them across forty Services, instead of building forty bespoke stacks.

Key Takeaways

  • One Layer 3 image serves many Layer 2 Services; bytes are reused, runtime parameters specialize.
  • Three parameter sources: image-baked defaults, Container App env vars, Microsoft.KeyVault-backed secrets — in increasing scope.
  • Across the 38-Service catalog, ~8–10 reusable agent images plus ~10 practice-specific specializations cover the operational core.
  • Fork an image when logic changes fundamentally; never to change a config value.

Deployment Drills

  1. For one of your Lab agents, list the env vars and Key Vault secrets it consumes. Mark which would change if the same image deployed into HLS-E2E-04 instead of RC-E2E-03.
  2. From Appendix E, pick three Services in three different practices that all compose The Analyst. Sketch the per-Service config bundle each would need.
  3. Audit your authoring registry's image list. Count how many images you have and how many Services they collectively serve. If the ratio is below 1:2, ask whether you have over-specialized the agent set.
Chapter 11.5

Deploying Microsoft Agent Framework Agents

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Walks the full deployment lifecycle for an Agent Framework agent on Microsoft Foundry Agent Service — from container image requirements to managed-identity assignment to A2A endpoint exposure.
  • Contrasts the two production deployment models (Container Apps direct · Foundry Hosted Agents) and shows when each is the right fit per Service.
  • Maps the Bicep modules (infra/bicep/modules/agent-fleet.bicep, infra/bicep/platform/identity.bicep) and the azd commands that drive build / push / deploy / version.
  • Specifies observability, network isolation, scaling, and promotion (Lab → DEV → STAGE → PROD) so the agent is operable from day one of go-live.
90-second read
  • Two deployment models. Container Apps direct (full control · A2A via MapA2A on the apex-m host · APEX manages scaling and identity) versus Foundry Hosted Agents (Microsoft-managed runtime · auto-scaling · auto-injected env vars · platform-managed Entra agent identity). Default for production: Foundry Hosted Agents.
  • Container image must be linux/amd64 (build on Apple Silicon: docker build --platform linux/amd64 .) and import a Foundry protocol library (azure-ai-agentserver-responses or azure-ai-agentserver-invocations) to communicate with the Foundry gateway.
  • Lifecycle: build & push to Microsoft.ContainerRegistry · register version with Foundry Agent Service · poll for active · invoke via /responses or /invocations. azd provision + azd deploy runs it end-to-end.
  • Identity is per-agent: every deployed agent gets a dedicated Microsoft Entra ID identity assigned by Foundry. Bicep grants per-Service role assignments to that identity (Microsoft.KeyVault secrets · Microsoft Fabric Gold · Microsoft.Cache/Redis · APEX MCP host).
  • Observability via the auto-injected APPLICATIONINSIGHTS_CONNECTION_STRING; OpenTelemetry env vars carry agent ID, Service code, three-version stamp as resource attributes — every span is queryable per Service per agent per version.

11.5.1 Two Deployment Models

APEX-M supports two production deployment models for Agent Framework agents:

Container Apps directFoundry Hosted Agents
Microsoft resource typeMicrosoft.App/containerAppsFoundry Agent Service (managed) — backed by container image in Microsoft.ContainerRegistry
Runtime managementAPEX team owns scaling, restarts, revisionsMicrosoft-managed; platform handles scaling, session state, lifecycle
IdentityUser-assigned managed identity (per-agent)Foundry-issued Microsoft Entra agent identity (per-agent, auto-provisioned)
Endpoint protocolHTTP per /run (FastAPI) + A2A via MapA2AFoundry Responses (/responses) or Invocations (/invocations) — OpenAI-compatible
Session stateAPEX manages via Microsoft.Cache/Redis (§14.6.6)Platform persists $HOME and uploaded files across turns
Best forCustom protocols · sub-second latency · co-located orchestrationMulti-tenant operability · zero-ops · OpenAI-compatible client SDK access
APEX-M defaultReserved for laptop / Lab v0.1 (Docker Compose)DEV / STAGE / PROD (per ADR-006 hosting policy)

The Loader (Services Guide §14.5.8) is unchanged across both models — only the host wrapper differs. The same build_workflow() output is mounted under FastAPI's MapA2A for Container Apps and registered as the agent's request handler for Foundry Hosted.

Required Microsoft Entra roles — must be assigned before deployment

Per Microsoft Learn (deploy a hosted agent, Entra built-in roles), the engagement principal performing the deploy must hold these roles before azd up will succeed:

Role (Entra built-in or RBAC)ScopeWhy APEX needs it
Azure AI Project ManagerFoundry project (resource scope)Create / version / publish hosted agents in the Foundry project; required for every azd-driven deploy
Agent ID AdministratorMicrosoft Entra tenantCreate and manage agent identity blueprints tenant-wide (used by the Layer 1 Foundry agent identity bootstrap in §5A.7)
Agent ID DeveloperPer-blueprint (delegated scope)Author and version blueprints for a specific Service without tenant-wide privilege (engagement-team default — pairs with Conditional Access governing the principal)
AcrPushMicrosoft.ContainerRegistry · APEX shared registryPush the signed linux/amd64 image to ACR (§12.3 Notation signing pipeline writes here)
Key Vault Crypto UserMicrosoft.KeyVault · signing-key vaultUse the Notation signing key in Microsoft.KeyVault Premium HSM to sign the image (per §12.3, ADR-006 promotion gate)
ReaderResource group containing Foundry project + Layer 1 platformRead existing platform identity, network, observability resources Bicep references with existing

Engagement teams that hit "insufficient privilege" failures during azd up almost always lack Azure AI Project Manager — it is project-scoped and not granted automatically by tenant-wide Contributor roles. PIM-eligible activation per §5.3 is the production path; permanent assignment is restricted to Lab v0.1.

11.5.2 Container Image Requirements

Per the hosted-agent deployment guide, the container image must satisfy:

  • Architecture: linux/amd64. ARM-built images (Apple Silicon default) will fail to start. Build with docker build --platform linux/amd64 . or use a multi-arch CI runner.
  • Protocol library imported. Choose Responses (conversational, streaming, multi-turn) or Invocations (webhook, async, custom payloads). A single container can expose both by declaring both in agent.yaml and importing both libraries.
  • Health probe at /healthz. APEX's contract from §10.1 — Foundry's load probes hit this endpoint before routing requests.
  • Listen on port 8088 (Foundry default) or ${PORT} if Foundry overrides at runtime.
  • No platform-injected env vars in agent.yaml. The platform sets FOUNDRY_PROJECT_ENDPOINT, FOUNDRY_AGENT_NAME, FOUNDRY_AGENT_VERSION, FOUNDRY_AGENT_SESSION_ID, APPLICATIONINSIGHTS_CONNECTION_STRING. Do not redeclare them.
apps/agents/the-pricer/Dockerfile · APEX-M canonical
FROM python:3.12-slim AS base
WORKDIR /app

# Build args from azd / CI
ARG AGENT_ID
ARG MANIFEST_VERSION
ARG POLICY_VERSION
ARG PROMPT_VERSION

ENV AGENT_ID=${AGENT_ID} \
    MANIFEST_VERSION=${MANIFEST_VERSION} \
    POLICY_VERSION=${POLICY_VERSION} \
    PROMPT_VERSION=${PROMPT_VERSION} \
    PORT=8088

COPY pyproject.toml ./
RUN pip install --no-cache-dir ".[runtime]"   # pulls agent-framework + Foundry provider

COPY src/ ./src/
COPY agent.yaml prompts/ ./

EXPOSE 8088
CMD ["uvicorn", "apex_m.host:app", "--host", "0.0.0.0", "--port", "8088"]

11.5.3 Foundry Hosted Agent Lifecycle

Five-step lifecycle per deploy a hosted agent:

  1. Build & push. CI builds the linux/amd64 image · tags with version · pushes to Microsoft.ContainerRegistry (§4.3 of this book). Microsoft Notation signature applied per §12.3.
  2. Create version. azd (or REST POST /agents/{name}/versions) registers the image with Foundry Agent Service. Foundry provisions infrastructure and assigns the per-agent Entra identity.
  3. Poll for status. Wait until version status reaches active. Typical: 60–180 seconds.
  4. Invoke. Send requests to the dedicated endpoint: POST {project}/agents/{name}/endpoint/protocols/openai/responses for the Responses protocol or .../protocols/invocations for Invocations.
  5. New version on update. Each Bicep deploy that changes image SHA / agent.yaml / Bicep parameters publishes a new version. Foundry routes new traffic to active versions; old versions remain available for rollback per Service-Bicep configuration.

11.5.4 Platform-Injected Environment Variables

Foundry injects these at runtime — agent code reads them but never sets them:

VariablePurpose · APEX use
FOUNDRY_PROJECT_ENDPOINTFoundry project endpoint URL · used by FoundryChatClient to route inference
FOUNDRY_PROJECT_ARM_IDProject ARM resource ID · used for cross-resource role-assignment lookups
FOUNDRY_AGENT_NAMERunning agent name · APEX writes to LEDGER row's agent_id column for cross-validation
FOUNDRY_AGENT_VERSIONRunning version · stamps every LEDGER row alongside manifest / policy / prompt versions (the four-stamp audit anchor)
FOUNDRY_AGENT_SESSION_IDPer-request session ID · used as the conversation key for the Redis RedisHistoryProvider (Services Guide §14.6.6)
APPLICATIONINSIGHTS_CONNECTION_STRINGAuto-wired by Foundry to the project's Application Insights resource · OpenTelemetry exports go here zero-code
AZURE_AI_MODEL_DEPLOYMENT_NAMEDefault model deployment per azd ai agent init · Cost-envelope router can override per Service

APEX-declared variables (set in agent.yaml's environment_variables section, not in code):

agent.yaml · APEX-side vars (alongside agent metadata)
name: rc-e2e-03-the-pricer
description: Generate markdown proposals for cold-chain excursions.
model: gpt-4o
environment_variables:
  - name: AGENT_ID
    value: the-pricer
  - name: SERVICE_CODE
    value: RC-E2E-03
  - name: MANIFEST_VERSION
    value: 3.2.1
  - name: POLICY_VERSION
    value: 2.4.0
  - name: PROMPT_VERSION
    value: 1.7.3
  - name: APEX_MCP_BASE_URL
    secret_ref: apex-mcp-base-url-rc-e2e-03
  - name: APEX_REDIS_URL
    secret_ref: apex-redis-url
toolboxes:
  - apex-rc-e2e-03-mcp                # hosted MCP toolbox in this Foundry project

11.5.5 azd Provision + Deploy Flow

Azure Developer CLI is the canonical APEX deployment tool. Required: azd ext install azure.ai.agents.

azd · APEX-M agent deployment (per Service)
# One-time provisioning per Lab / DEV / STAGE / PROD environment
azd env new apex-rc-e2e-03-prod
azd provision                # creates Foundry project · model deployment ·
                             # App Insights · Microsoft.ContainerRegistry · KV

# Deploy a single agent (idempotent; creates new version on each call)
azd deploy --service the-pricer

# Deploy the full Service fleet (uses azure.yaml service map)
azd deploy

# Inspect status
azd ai agent show --name rc-e2e-03-the-pricer
azd ai agent versions --name rc-e2e-03-the-pricer

The azure.yaml at the repo root maps APEX agents to Foundry agent definitions:

azure.yaml · service-to-agent mapping (APEX-M)
name: apex-m-rc-e2e-03
metadata:
  template: apex-m-foundry-hosted
services:
  the-pricer:
    project: apps/agents/the-pricer
    language: python
    host: ai.agent
    config:
      agent_yaml: ./agent.yaml
  the-demand-checker:
    project: apps/agents/the-demand-checker
    language: python
    host: ai.agent
    config:
      agent_yaml: ./agent.yaml
  # ... one block per agent in the Service fleet

11.5.6 Agent Identity — Per-Agent Microsoft Entra ID

Foundry Hosted Agents assign each version a dedicated Microsoft Entra ID. APEX exploits this for fine-grained authorization — the per-agent identity is what holds the role assignments in the Service Bicep:

infra/bicep/platform/identity.bicep · per-agent role assignments
// Foundry assigns the agent identity automatically; we look it up by agent
// principal-id output from the Foundry agent resource and grant per-service rights.

resource thePricerKvSecretsUser 'Microsoft.Authorization/roleAssignments@2022-04-01' = {
  scope: keyVault
  name: guid(keyVault.id, foundryAgentThePricerPrincipalId, 'kv-secrets-user')
  properties: {
    roleDefinitionId: subscriptionResourceId(
      'Microsoft.Authorization/roleDefinitions',
      '4633458b-17de-408a-b874-0445c86b69e6'   // Key Vault Secrets User
    )
    principalId: foundryAgentThePricerPrincipalId
    principalType: 'ServicePrincipal'
  }
}

resource thePricerFabricReader 'Microsoft.Authorization/roleAssignments@2022-04-01' = {
  scope: fabricCapacity
  name: guid(fabricCapacity.id, foundryAgentThePricerPrincipalId, 'gold-mart-reader')
  properties: {
    roleDefinitionId: subscriptionResourceId(
      'Microsoft.Authorization/roleDefinitions',
      'acdd72a7-3385-48ef-bd42-f606fba81ae7'   // Reader on Fabric workspace
    )
    principalId: foundryAgentThePricerPrincipalId
    principalType: 'ServicePrincipal'
  }
}

This per-agent identity model is the foundation for the four-tier PII classification — only the pii-unlock identity (a separate user-assigned managed identity, dual-controlled in Microsoft.KeyVault) can decrypt Tier-3 PHI. The agent identity proper has read-only access to Bronze tokenized data and Gold mart views.

11.5.7 The agent-fleet.bicep Module

The Service Bicep composes the agent fleet via infra/bicep/modules/agent-fleet.bicep. One Bicep call per agent in the Service:

infra/bicep/blueprints/w1-foundation.bicep · agent fleet per Service
module thePricer 'modules/agent-fleet.bicep' = {
  name: 'agent-the-pricer-${serviceCode}'
  params: {
    agentName: 'the-pricer'
    serviceCode: serviceCode
    foundryProjectName: foundryProject.name
    imageReference: '${acr.properties.loginServer}/apex-m/the-pricer:${imageVersion}'
    manifestVersion: manifestVersion
    policyVersion: policyVersion
    promptVersion: promptVersion
    keyVaultName: keyVault.name
    redisName: redis.name
    mcpHostUrl: mcpHost.properties.fqdn
    appInsightsConnectionString: appInsights.properties.ConnectionString
    minReplicas: 1
    maxReplicas: 10
    cpu: '0.5'
    memory: '1Gi'
  }
}

The module declares the Foundry agent resource (Microsoft.MachineLearningServices/workspaces/agents in current API) with version, image reference, environment variables, and toolbox bindings. It also outputs the per-agent principal ID for downstream identity Bicep to consume.

11.5.8 A2A Endpoint Exposure

For Container Apps direct deployments, A2A is enabled by mounting the Agent Framework hosting middleware on the apex-m FastAPI app:

apex_m/host.py · A2A exposure on Container Apps
from fastapi import FastAPI
from agent_framework.hosting.a2a import map_a2a
from apex_m.agent_framework_loader import build_workflow

app = FastAPI()

# Loader-built workflow exposed as A2A
workflow = build_workflow(use_case_yaml="agent.yaml")
map_a2a(app, workflow, route="/a2a/the-pricer")

# Agent card auto-published at /.well-known/agent-card.json
# A2A clients can now discover and invoke via HTTP+JSON

For Foundry Hosted Agents, A2A exposure is automatic — Foundry Agent Service publishes the agent card and routes both Responses and A2A protocols on the dedicated agent endpoint. APEX-M's azure.yaml declares expose_a2a: true in the agent's config section.

Warning — cross-tenant A2A is forbidden

Per ADR-025-01 (Services Guide §31.4), A2A traffic must stay within the client's Microsoft Entra ID tenant. The agent-fleet Bicep enforces this by adding an Entra ID claim check to the Container Apps ingress and by requiring a tenant-bound app registration for the A2A client. Cross-tenant calls fail at the ingress, not at the agent.

11.5.9 Health Probes, Scaling, and KEDA Rules

Container Apps direct deployments use KEDA-style scaling rules. Recommended for APEX agents:

TriggerBicep / Container App configWhy
HTTP concurrent requestsscale.rules: [{ name: http, http: { metadata: { concurrentRequests: '20' } } }]Agent calls are bursty; 20-req concurrency per replica matches typical model-call latency
Min replicas: 1scale.minReplicas: 1Cold-start penalty for an agent is the LLM warm-up plus context-provider init — keep one warm
Max replicas: 10 (default) or per-Servicescale.maxReplicas: 10Cost envelope (§30.1.6) governs the ceiling; over-provisioning agents is the most common Lab→PROD cost surprise
Liveness probe/healthz · 200 = OKFoundry routes only to versions whose probes pass
Startup probe/healthz with 60s grace periodLoader's lazy-import (§14.5.8.2) plus model-deployment warm-up takes 30–45s on cold start

Foundry Hosted Agents scale automatically — the platform decides replica count from concurrent-session metrics. APEX configures only the per-agent CPU / memory / max-concurrent-sessions bounds via agent.yaml's resources section.

11.5.9.1 Foundry Hosted-Agent Sandbox SKUs

Foundry hosted agents run in a managed sandbox; the resources block in agent.yaml selects one of the per-Microsoft-Learn SKUs:

SKUvCPUMemoryCold-start (typical)APEX best fit
XS0.250.5 GiB~30 sLightweight classifier / router agents (e.g., The Briefer trigger filter, ICE prompt-policy router)
S0.51 GiB~35 sStandard reasoning agents · most APEX-M Service agents default here (The Pricer, The Demand Checker)
M12 GiB~45 sRAG-heavy agents with larger context windows · HLS-E2E-04 prior-auth · ER-E2E-02 claims-denial
L24 GiB~60 sMulti-tool agents and long-running plans · The Analyst when it runs as Magentic manager · TH (TestHarness) replay agents

Concurrency caps differ by SKU — XS supports few-to-a-handful of concurrent sessions; L supports tens. Per Microsoft Learn, the platform enforces a per-version concurrency limit derived from the SKU. APEX practice: start at S for every agent and promote to M only when Application Insights shows sustained memory pressure or context-window-driven slowness. L is reserved for Magentic managers and TH replay; XS is reserved for stateless routers.

Best Practice — pin the SKU in agent.yaml, not in Bicep

The sandbox SKU is an agent-level concern, not a Service-platform concern. Declaring it in agent.yaml lets the engagement engineer tune the SKU without redeploying Layer 1 platform Bicep. Sample shape:

agent.yaml · sandbox SKU declaration
name: rc-e2e-03-the-pricer
model: gpt-4o
resources:
  sandbox_sku: S          # XS | S | M | L  (per Microsoft Learn)
  max_concurrent_sessions: 12

11.5.10 Observability Wiring — OpenTelemetry + Application Insights

Three layers of telemetry compose:

  • Foundry-injected. APPLICATIONINSIGHTS_CONNECTION_STRING is auto-set; the Agent Framework SDK auto-instruments traces, metrics, logs to Application Insights. Zero APEX code.
  • APEX resource attributes. Set via OTEL_RESOURCE_ATTRIBUTES in agent.yaml's env vars: service.name=apex-m-the-pricer,service.namespace=rc-e2e-03,apex.manifest_version=3.2.1,apex.policy_version=2.4.0,apex.prompt_version=1.7.3. Every span carries these — KQL queries pivot per Service · per agent · per version trivially.
  • APEX Audit Middleware. Per Services Guide §14.6.5, ApexAuditMiddleware emits a LEDGER row per invocation. The LEDGER is anchored separately (Microsoft.KeyVault HSM CMK · WORM blob storage) and complements telemetry — telemetry tells you what happened operationally, LEDGER tells you what happened forensically.
KQL — pivot agent telemetry by version (Application Insights Logs)
AppTraces
| where TimeGenerated > ago(24h)
| where Properties has "service.namespace"
| extend service_code = tostring(Properties["service.namespace"])
| extend agent_id = tostring(Properties["service.name"])
| extend prompt_version = tostring(Properties["apex.prompt_version"])
| summarize
    count(),
    p95_latency_ms = percentile(DurationMs, 95),
    error_rate = countif(SeverityLevel > 2) * 1.0 / count()
  by service_code, agent_id, prompt_version
| order by service_code asc, agent_id asc, prompt_version desc

11.5.11 Network Isolation — Private Endpoints & VNet

STAGE and PROD topologies put every Microsoft platform component behind private endpoints inside the APEX-M VNet (per Ch 4 of this book). Agent-relevant flows:

HopSourceDestinationMechanism
Agent → Foundry inferenceContainer Apps direct: VNet-integrated Container App
Foundry Hosted: Foundry-managed network		Microsoft Foundry project endpointPrivate endpoint on Microsoft.MachineLearningServices/workspaces · DNS via Private DNS Zone
Agent → APEX MCP hostSame VNetMCP host on Container AppsContainer Apps internal ingress · Entra ID claim auth · APEX MCP managed identity
Agent → Microsoft.Cache/RedisSame VNetRedis Premium + private endpointPrivate endpoint · Entra ID auth · APEX runtime managed identity
Agent → Microsoft.KeyVaultSame VNetKey Vault Premium HSMPrivate endpoint · per-agent role assignment from §11.5.6
A2A peer-to-peerSame tenantPeer agent's A2A endpointContainer Apps internal ingress (Container Apps direct) · Foundry agent endpoint (Foundry Hosted)
⚠ Current constraint — ACR must be reachable over its public endpoint for hosted-agent deploy

Per Microsoft Learn (check for updates — Microsoft has signaled private-endpoint support is on the roadmap), Foundry hosted-agent deployment currently pulls the image from Azure Container Registry over the public endpoint. If your APEX-M deployment locks ACR to private-endpoint-only (the Lab→PROD "private by default" posture in §12A), azd up will hang at the agent-version creation step.

Two supported workarounds:

  1. Service-endpoint exception. On the ACR network rules, add the Foundry service tag (AzureCognitiveServices and AzureMachineLearning regional service tags cover the current Foundry control plane) to the allow list. Keeps everything else private; opens just the Foundry pull path.
  2. Stage via a public-reachable mirror ACR. Push to a small public-endpoint-enabled ACR for the Foundry deploy step, then continue to serve runtime pulls from your private ACR (the agent container is already running by then and does not need ACR after start).

APEX-M's infra/bicep/platform/acr.bicep exposes a foundryDeployServiceTagException parameter (default true on DEV / false on STAGE/PROD) so engagement teams can pick the trade-off explicitly. Track hosted agents concept for GA of private-endpoint support; remove the exception once that ships.

11.5.12 Promotion — Lab v0.1 → DEV → STAGE → PROD

The Bicep blueprint in infra/bicep/blueprints/ covers the four substrate tiers. Agent-specific differences across tiers:

TierHostingIdentityImage sourceTelemetry
Laptop (free)Docker Compose · n8n + apex-m + agent-framework-loaded agents (lazy-import path)Local-only; no EntraLocal buildConsole logs · OTel to local Aspire dashboard
Lab v0.1Container Apps direct · single resource groupUser-assigned managed identity per agent (manual Bicep)Microsoft.ContainerRegistry · Lab-tier SKUApp Insights (Lab project)
DEVFoundry Hosted Agents · DEV Foundry projectFoundry-issued Entra agent identitiesMicrosoft.ContainerRegistry · Microsoft Notation signature requiredApp Insights (DEV) + LEDGER (DEV)
STAGEFoundry Hosted Agents · STAGE Foundry project · VNet integrationFoundry-issued Entra agent identities · Defender-for-Identity monitoringPromoted by SHA from DEV registryApp Insights (STAGE) + LEDGER (STAGE) · 7-day retention
PRODFoundry Hosted Agents · PROD Foundry project · full VNet/private-endpoint topology · paired regionsFoundry-issued Entra agent identities · conditional access · privileged-access workstation requirement for break-glassPromoted by SHA from STAGE registry · cosign attestation gateApp Insights (PROD) + LEDGER (PROD · Microsoft.KeyVault HSM CMK · WORM)

Image SHA promotion (not rebuild) is the key invariant — the bytes that ran in DEV are the bytes that run in PROD, with environment-specific behavior driven entirely by Bicep parameters and platform-injected env vars (§11.5.4). Per ADR-006 / §12.3, Microsoft Notation signature plus cosign attestation form the promotion gate; STAGE → PROD without a valid attestation chain is blocked at the Bicep deploy step.

11.5.13 Cross-References

  • Foundry hosted agents (.NET / Python) · hosted agents concept · deployment how-to
  • Quickstart — create and deploy a hosted agent · VS Code workflow
  • Foundry Agent Service overview (three agent types: Prompt · Workflow · Hosted)
  • A2A hosting (.NET ASP.NET Core) · Python A2AExecutor
  • Agent observability · GenAI semantic conventions
  • Azure Developer CLI (azd) · azd ext install azure.ai.agents
  • This book: Ch 4 — APEX Platform · Ch 10 — Layer 3 Agent · Ch 11 — Agent Reuse · Ch 12 — Versioning
  • Services Guide: §14.5.8 — APEX-M Agent Framework Loader · Ch 14B — Microsoft Agent Framework Deep Dive · §31 — Microsoft Agent Stack

Key Takeaways

  • Two deployment models — Container Apps direct (full control · Lab v0.1) and Foundry Hosted Agents (managed · DEV/STAGE/PROD default per ADR-006).
  • Container image must be linux/amd64 with a Foundry protocol library (Responses or Invocations); listen on port 8088; never redeclare platform-injected env vars.
  • Lifecycle is build → push → register version → poll active → invoke. azd provision + azd deploy automates it; azure.yaml maps APEX agents to Foundry agents.
  • Each deployed agent gets a dedicated Microsoft Entra ID identity assigned by Foundry; APEX Bicep grants per-Service role assignments to it (Microsoft.KeyVault · Microsoft Fabric Gold · Microsoft.Cache/Redis · APEX MCP).
  • Observability is OpenTelemetry-native; APPLICATIONINSIGHTS_CONNECTION_STRING is platform-injected; APEX adds resource attributes for the four-stamp audit anchor (manifest · policy · prompt · agent version).
  • STAGE / PROD use private endpoints throughout · A2A stays within the Microsoft Entra tenant per ADR-025-01 · cross-tenant calls fail at the ingress.
  • Image SHA promotion (not rebuild) Lab → DEV → STAGE → PROD; Microsoft Notation + cosign attestation form the deploy gate.

Deployment Drills

  1. For one of your Lab agents, write the agent.yaml environment_variables and toolboxes blocks. Mark which env vars are APEX-declared vs platform-injected. Validate with azd ai agent show.
  2. Trace the per-agent identity from Bicep deploy through to a Microsoft.KeyVault secret read. Identify every role assignment the agent needs and confirm none are over-broad.
  3. Run a STAGE → PROD promotion of one Service. Verify image SHA is unchanged · attestation chain validates · App Insights queries find the new agent version emitting telemetry within 5 minutes of the version going active.
  4. Force a cold start (scale to zero, then trigger). Measure the time to first /run response. If > 60s, identify whether the bottleneck is image pull, Loader lazy-import, model-deployment warm-up, or context-provider init — and which scaling rule to adjust.
Chapter 11.6

Deploying Responsible AI Controls

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Provisions Azure AI Content Safety · wires content-shield middleware into APEX-M agents · deploys per-Service custom categories.
  • Wires the three-gate evaluation framework — golden-set regression · LLM-as-judge sampling · canary metrics — into the deployment pipeline as promotion gates.
  • Publishes per-Service model cards as part of Bicep deploys.
60-second read
  • Azure AI Content Safety is provisioned via Microsoft.CognitiveServices/accounts (kind=ContentSafety); per-environment instance.
  • Custom categories deploy via REST after the account is provisioned; APEX-M ships deployment-scripts/content-safety-categories.ps1.
  • Eval gates are Foundry batch-evaluation runs · Bicep deployment scripts invoke them and check for green status before STAGE → PROD promotion.
  • Model cards live in apps/services/<service>/model-card.md · published to a per-Service SharePoint site as part of the Bicep deploy.

11.6.1 Azure AI Content Safety Bicep

infra/bicep/platform/content-safety.bicep
resource contentSafety 'Microsoft.CognitiveServices/accounts@2024-10-01' = {
  name: 'cs-${engagementCode}-${tier}'
  location: location
  kind: 'ContentSafety'
  sku: { name: tier == 'prod' ? 'S0' : 'F0' }
  properties: {
    customSubDomainName: 'cs-${engagementCode}-${tier}'
    publicNetworkAccess: tier == 'prod' ? 'Disabled' : 'Enabled'
    networkAcls: { defaultAction: 'Deny' }
    disableLocalAuth: true   // Entra ID auth only
  }
  identity: { type: 'SystemAssigned' }
}

output endpoint string = contentSafety.properties.endpoint
output principalId string = contentSafety.identity.principalId

Per-Service agent identity granted Cognitive Services User on the account so the Loader-built middleware can call it without secrets.

11.6.2 Custom Category Training

deployment-scripts/content-safety-categories.ps1
param(
  [string]$Endpoint,
  [string]$ServiceCode,
  [string]$CategoriesYamlPath
)

$cs = ConvertFrom-Yaml (Get-Content $CategoriesYamlPath -Raw)
$token = (Get-AzAccessToken -ResourceUrl 'https://cognitiveservices.azure.com').Token

foreach ($cat in $cs.categories) {
  $body = @{
    categoryName = "$ServiceCode-$($cat.name)"
    definition = $cat.definition
    sampleBlocklistItems = $cat.examples
  } | ConvertTo-Json -Depth 4

  Invoke-RestMethod -Method PUT `
    -Uri "${Endpoint}/contentsafety/text/categories/${ServiceCode}-$($cat.name)?api-version=2024-09-01" `
    -Headers @{ Authorization = "Bearer $token" } `
    -Body $body -ContentType 'application/json'
}

Example custom category YAML for HLS-E2E-04 prior-auth (shipped under apps/services/hls-e2e-04/content-safety-categories.yaml):

apps/services/hls-e2e-04/content-safety-categories.yaml
categories:
  - name: off-label-drug-suggestion
    definition: |
      Suggesting medication use outside FDA-approved indications.
    examples:
      - "Try [drug X] for [unapproved condition Y]"
      - "Off-label use for [condition]"
  - name: identifying-minor-without-guardian
    definition: |
      Recording or processing PHI of a minor without explicit guardian consent.
    examples:
      - "Patient is 14 years old, parent not present"

11.6.3 Eval Gate Bicep Deployment Script

infra/bicep/platform/eval-gate.bicep · invoked at promotion
resource evalGate 'Microsoft.Resources/deploymentScripts@2023-08-01' = {
  name: 'ds-eval-gate-${serviceCode}-${tier}'
  location: location
  kind: 'AzurePowerShell'
  identity: { type: 'UserAssigned'; userAssignedIdentities: { '${platformIdentityId}': {} } }
  properties: {
    azPowerShellVersion: '11.0'
    timeout: 'PT30M'
    retentionInterval: 'P1D'
    scriptContent: '''
      param([string]$ProjectEndpoint, [string]$ServiceCode, [string]$Tier)

      # Run Foundry batch evaluation against golden set
      $eval = Invoke-RestMethod -Method POST `
        -Uri "${ProjectEndpoint}/evaluations?api-version=2024-12-01-preview" `
        -Headers @{ Authorization = "Bearer $(Get-AzAccessToken -ResourceUrl 'https://ai.azure.com').Token" } `
        -Body (@{
          dataset = "golden-set-${ServiceCode}"
          evaluators = @('groundedness', 'relevance', 'coherence', 'safety')
          minThreshold = if ($Tier -eq 'prod') { 0.85 } else { 0.75 }
        } | ConvertTo-Json) `
        -ContentType 'application/json'

      # Block promotion if any evaluator is below threshold
      if ($eval.summary.failed -gt 0) {
        throw "Eval gate FAILED for ${ServiceCode} -> ${Tier}: $($eval.summary.failed) evaluators below threshold"
      }

      Write-Output "Eval gate PASSED for ${ServiceCode} -> ${Tier}"
    '''
    arguments: '-ProjectEndpoint ${foundryProjectEndpoint} -ServiceCode ${serviceCode} -Tier ${tier}'
  }
}

11.6.4 Model Card Publication

Each Service's model-card.md is part of the repo. Bicep deploys it to a per-Service SharePoint site at promotion:

deployment-scripts/publish-model-card.ps1
param([string]$ServiceCode, [string]$Tier, [string]$SharePointSiteUrl)
$modelCardPath = "apps/services/${ServiceCode}/model-card.md"
Connect-PnPOnline -Url $SharePointSiteUrl -ManagedIdentity
Add-PnPFile -Path $modelCardPath -Folder "Shared Documents/APEX-M/${ServiceCode}/${Tier}"

  • Services Guide Ch 14C — Responsible AI · §28.5 — three-gate evaluation

Key Takeaways

  • Content Safety provisioned via Microsoft.CognitiveServices/accounts kind=ContentSafety; system-assigned identity; Entra-only auth.
  • Custom categories per Service deploy via REST from YAML definitions in the repo.
  • Eval gates run as Bicep deploymentScripts invoking Foundry batch evaluations; block promotion on threshold miss.

Chapter 12

Versioning and Compatibility

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Establishes SemVer per layer — apex-platform, rc-e2e-03 (and other Services), agent-the-analyst (and other agents) — and what changes constitute major / minor / patch.
  • Defines the compatibility matrix that pins a Service version to compatible agent ranges and Platform ranges.
  • Covers the cosign attestation that travels with each agent image carrying the Microsoft Notation signature plus the platform-compatibility metadata.
60-second read
  • Three SemVer lines (apex-platform · per-Service · per-agent), each with its own cadence and major-bump triggers.
  • Compatibility matrix per Service version pins compatible agent ranges and Platform ranges; image SHA digests in Bicep lock a specific verified combination.
  • Microsoft Notation + cosign attestations carry platform-compatibility metadata enforceable at Service deploy time.
  • Cadence falls as you move down the cake: agents continuous · Services per upgrade window · Platform quarterly.

12.1 Three SemVer Lines

Each layer carries its own SemVer:

LayerVersioned thingMajor bump onMinor bump onPatch bump on
1apex-platformMicrosoft API-version upgrade requiring resource recreate; Layer 2 contract changeNew Layer 1 Microsoft resource added (Microsoft.Network additions, etc.)Bug fix; tag/label tweak
2per-Service (e.g., rc-e2e-03)KPI envelope redefinition; agent-set change requiring re-onboardingNew scenario added to Service; new agent compositionBicep parameter tweak; secret rename
3per-agent (e.g., agent-the-analyst)HTTP contract change at /run; LEDGER row schema changeNew MCP tool dependency; runner backward-compatible behavior changePrompt tweak; weight retune

The discipline that makes this work is asymmetric: Layer 3 patch / minor changes deploy continuously; Layer 2 minor changes deploy at Service onboarding; Layer 1 minor changes deploy quarterly. Cadence falls as you move down the cake. Major bumps at any layer are explicit, planned events.

12.2 The Compatibility Matrix

Each Service version declares the agent versions it is compatible with and the Platform versions it requires. The matrix lives in the Service's repository as COMPATIBILITY.md:

YAML — compatibility matrix excerpt (rc-e2e-03 v1.0.0)
service: rc-e2e-03
service_version: 1.0.0
platform_compatible: ">=0.1.0,<0.3.0"   # apex-platform
agents_compatible:
  the-analyst:         ">=1.4.0,<2.0.0"
  the-demand-checker:  ">=0.8.0,<1.0.0"
  the-finance-lead:    ">=0.7.0,<1.0.0"
  the-operations-lead: ">=1.2.0,<2.0.0"
  the-briefer:         ">=0.5.0,<1.0.0"
verified_combinations:
  - { platform: 0.1.0, agents: { the-analyst: 1.4.0, ... } }
  - { platform: 0.2.1, agents: { the-analyst: 1.4.2, ... } }

The Service Bicep parameter file references specific image SHA digests (Chapter 8); the compatibility matrix records the version range within which those digests are known to interoperate. A new agent minor version that falls within range can be promoted to a Service deployment without changing the Service version. A new agent major version requires a Service minor or major bump.

12.3 The Attestation Trail

Microsoft Notation signatures and cosign attestations travel with each image as ORAS artifacts. The cosign attestation can carry arbitrary in-toto predicates; the APEX pipeline writes a predicate of type apex.deloitte/agent-attestation with:

  • Image SHA digest
  • Agent SemVer + git commit SHA of source
  • Platform-compatibility range
  • Microsoft Defender for Containers scan summary (assessment ID + result)
  • Build timestamp + Microsoft.ContainerRegistry task identifier

The Service Bicep deployment can verify the attestation at deploy time, refusing to bind an agent image whose attested platform-compatibility range does not include the current Layer 1 version. That makes Layer 1/2/3 compatibility an enforceable runtime check, not a documentation request.

12.4 What Each Layer's Lifecycle Looks Like in Practice

LayerCadenceTriggerApprovalDeploy mechanism
3 (Agent)Continuous (per merge to main)PR-driven build + Microsoft Defender scan + Notation signCI greenMirror to client container registry by import; Service can pin or float within range
2 (Service)Per Service onboarding · per upgrade windowNew scenario added, new agent composition, KPI-envelope refinementService ownerBicep deployment of Service module against tenant subscription
1 (Platform)QuarterlyMicrosoft API-version updates, Microsoft Defender feature additions, regional expansionPlatform engineering + client architect signoffBicep main.bicep redeploy with what-if review

Key Takeaways

  • Three SemVer lines (apex-platform, per-Service, per-agent), each with its own cadence and major-bump triggers.
  • Compatibility matrix per Service version pins compatible agent ranges and Platform ranges; image SHA digests in Bicep lock a specific verified combination.
  • Microsoft Notation + cosign attestations carry platform-compatibility metadata enforceable at Service deploy time.
  • Cadence falls as you move down the cake: agents continuous, Services per upgrade window, Platform quarterly.

Deployment Drills

  1. Write the COMPATIBILITY.md for your current Service version. Confirm the agent ranges match the SHA digests pinned in Bicep.
  2. Pull the cosign attestation from one of your authoring-container registry agent images. Confirm the in-toto predicate carries platform-compatibility metadata.
  3. Walk one PR that touched Layer 3 only. Confirm no Layer 2 or Layer 1 version was bumped — and audit the merge if any was.
Part V

The Deployment Motion

Day 46. Priya's first client tenant is provisioned end-to-end. The image SHAs are pulled. The Service Bicep is deployed. The first synthetic excursion event has flowed all the way from a test trigger to a Microsoft Teams adaptive card and back. Everything she did to get here was a series of choices — but the playbook those choices instantiated is the same playbook that will run for the second client, and the third, and the tenth. The deployment motion is what makes Priya's engagement reproducible.

Four motions run in this order across every engagement, plus a fifth that activates only when something has gone wrong: (1) Day-Zero tenant onboarding — stand up Layer 1 in a fresh Azure subscription. (2) Add a Service — Layer 2 module onto an existing Layer 1, idempotently. (3) Agent upgrades — image SHA promotion across the Services that compose an agent, with canary traffic and observability gates. (4) Rollback and disaster recovery — the discipline that turns a bad deploy into 30 minutes of lost time instead of a weekend incident. (5) Failure scenarios and chaos engineering — preemptive testing so that when something fails in production, you've already proven the recovery path.

Five chapters: Ch 13 (Day-Zero) · Ch 14 (Add Service) · Ch 15 (Agent Upgrades) · Ch 16 (Rollback & DR) · Ch 16.5 (Failure Scenarios & Chaos). Each motion is documented as step · expected outcome · failure mode · recovery. Priya's next engagement — and the engagement after that — will follow the same five tracks. The motions are what convert "Priya knows how" into "Deloitte's Microsoft practice ships."

Chapters in this Part

  • Chapter 13Day-Zero Tenant Onboarding
  • Chapter 14Adding a Service to a Tenant
  • Chapter 15Agent Upgrades Across Clients
  • Chapter 16Rollback, DR, Reusable-Asset Discipline
  • Chapter 16.5Failure Scenarios & Chaos Engineering
Chapter 12A

Networking & Observability — Deep Dive

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Walks the full APEX-M network topology: hub-spoke VNet · subnets · NSGs · Azure Firewall · Front Door / App Gateway · private endpoints + private DNS zones.
  • Drills the observability stack: Microsoft.Insights / Log Analytics / OpenTelemetry / Microsoft Sentinel · which signals go where · what KQL queries the on-call uses daily.
60-second read
  • Hub-spoke VNet · APEX-M platform spoke peers to a hub holding shared firewall + DNS · per-Service workloads land in dedicated subnets.
  • Subnet pattern per Service: container-apps · private-endpoints · gateway · management.
  • Private endpoints for every Microsoft platform resource in STAGE+ (Microsoft.Storage · Microsoft.KeyVault · Microsoft.Fabric · Microsoft.Purview · Microsoft.CognitiveServices · Microsoft.MachineLearningServices).
  • Telemetry pipeline: OpenTelemetry (in agent code) → App Insights connection string → Log Analytics workspace → Sentinel.
  • One workspace per environment serves App Insights, Sentinel, container-app logs, Microsoft Fabric diagnostic logs — single pane of glass.

12A.1 Hub-Spoke VNet Topology

infra/bicep/network/topology.bicep · APEX-M hub-spoke
// Hub VNet (often pre-existing in client tenant; APEX-M peers into it)
resource hubVnet 'Microsoft.Network/virtualNetworks@2023-11-01' existing = {
  name: hubVnetName
  scope: resourceGroup(hubResourceGroup)
}

// APEX-M spoke VNet
resource spokeVnet 'Microsoft.Network/virtualNetworks@2023-11-01' = {
  name: 'vnet-apex-${engagementCode}-${tier}'
  location: location
  properties: {
    addressSpace: { addressPrefixes: ['10.${addressOctet}.0.0/16'] }
    subnets: [
      { name: 'container-apps'; properties: { addressPrefix: '10.${addressOctet}.0.0/23'; delegations: [{ name: 'Microsoft.App/environments'; properties: { serviceName: 'Microsoft.App/environments' } }] } }
      { name: 'private-endpoints'; properties: { addressPrefix: '10.${addressOctet}.2.0/24' } }
      { name: 'gateway'; properties: { addressPrefix: '10.${addressOctet}.3.0/24' } }
      { name: 'management'; properties: { addressPrefix: '10.${addressOctet}.4.0/24' } }
    ]
  }
}

// Hub ↔ spoke peering both directions
resource hubToSpoke 'Microsoft.Network/virtualNetworks/virtualNetworkPeerings@2023-11-01' = {
  parent: hubVnet
  name: 'to-apex-${engagementCode}-${tier}'
  properties: { remoteVirtualNetwork: { id: spokeVnet.id }; allowForwardedTraffic: true; allowGatewayTransit: true }
}
resource spokeToHub 'Microsoft.Network/virtualNetworks/virtualNetworkPeerings@2023-11-01' = {
  parent: spokeVnet
  name: 'to-hub'
  properties: { remoteVirtualNetwork: { id: hubVnet.id }; allowForwardedTraffic: true; useRemoteGateways: true }
}
