12A.3 The Observability Pipeline

Telemetry flow
[ APEX-M agent code ]
   ↓ OpenTelemetry SDK (zero-code via env vars)
[ App Insights connection string ]
   ↓
[ Microsoft.Insights/components ]
   ↓ (workspace-based; component points to LA workspace)
[ Microsoft.OperationalInsights/workspaces ]
   ↓
[ Microsoft Sentinel (same workspace) ]
   ↓
[ KQL · dashboards · alerts · automation rules ]

Parallel pipelines into the same workspace:
- Container Apps log stream (configured at managedEnvironment level)
- Microsoft Fabric diagnostic settings (per workspace)
- Microsoft.KeyVault diagnostic settings (per vault)
- Microsoft Entra ID diagnostic settings (SigninLogs · AuditLogs)
- Microsoft.App diagnostic settings (Container App requests/console)
- Azure Activity Log diagnostic settings (subscription level)

12A.4 Diagnostic Settings — Bicep

infra/bicep/platform/diagnostic-settings.bicep
// Container App managed environment → LA
resource cappDiag 'Microsoft.Insights/diagnosticSettings@2021-05-01-preview' = {
  scope: managedEnv
  name: 'to-log-analytics'
  properties: {
    workspaceId: logAnalyticsWorkspaceId
    logs: [
      { categoryGroup: 'allLogs'; enabled: true }
    ]
  }
}

// Microsoft.KeyVault diagnostic settings (audit ALL key vault access)
resource kvDiag 'Microsoft.Insights/diagnosticSettings@2021-05-01-preview' = {
  scope: keyVault
  name: 'to-log-analytics'
  properties: {
    workspaceId: logAnalyticsWorkspaceId
    logs: [
      { category: 'AuditEvent'; enabled: true }
      { category: 'AzurePolicyEvaluationDetails'; enabled: true }
    ]
    metrics: [{ category: 'AllMetrics'; enabled: true }]
  }
}

// Microsoft Entra ID diagnostic settings (tenant-scope; requires PIM-elevated deploy)
resource entraDiag 'Microsoft.Insights/diagnosticSettings@2021-05-01-preview' = {
  scope: tenant()
  name: 'apex-${engagementCode}-entra-to-la'
  properties: {
    workspaceId: logAnalyticsWorkspaceId
    logs: [
      { category: 'SignInLogs'; enabled: true }
      { category: 'NonInteractiveUserSignInLogs'; enabled: true }
      { category: 'ServicePrincipalSignInLogs'; enabled: true }
      { category: 'ManagedIdentitySignInLogs'; enabled: true }
      { category: 'AuditLogs'; enabled: true }
      { category: 'AgentSignInLogs'; enabled: true }   // new for agent identities
      { category: 'RiskyUsers'; enabled: true }
      { category: 'UserRiskEvents'; enabled: true }
    ]
  }
}

12A.5 Daily KQL — The On-Call's Six Queries

queries/on-call-daily.kql
// 1. Agent error rate by Service over last 4h
ApexLedger_CL
| where TimeGenerated > ago(4h)
| where kind_s == "agent.invocation"
| summarize total = count(), errors = countif(result_s == "error") by service_code_s, agent_id_s
| extend error_rate = errors * 1.0 / total
| where error_rate > 0.05
| order by error_rate desc

// 2. Cost per decision today (per Service)
ApexLedger_CL
| where TimeGenerated > startofday(now())
| where kind_s == "decision.completed"
| summarize decisions = count(), cost_usd = sum(cost_usd_d) by service_code_s
| extend cost_per_decision = cost_usd / decisions

// 3. Grounding score trend (last 7d, per agent)
ApexEval_CL
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| summarize avg_groundedness = avg(groundedness_score_d) by service_code_s, agent_id_s, bin(TimeGenerated, 1d)
| order by service_code_s, agent_id_s, TimeGenerated

// 4. Active Sentinel incidents (open + medium-and-above)
SecurityIncident
| where TimeGenerated > ago(24h)
| where Status in ("New", "Active")
| where Severity in ("High", "Medium")
| project IncidentNumber, Title, Severity, CreatedTime, Owner.assignedTo

// 5. Microsoft.Fabric capacity throttling events
AzureDiagnostics
| where TimeGenerated > ago(24h)
| where ResourceProvider == "MICROSOFT.FABRIC"
| where Category == "CapacityMetrics"
| where TelemetryType_s == "throttling"
| summarize events = count() by Resource

// 6. HITL approval response time (per Service · per persona)
ApexLedger_CL
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where kind_s == "hitl.completed"
| extend response_time_s = todatetime(approved_at_s) - todatetime(staged_at_s)
| summarize avg_response = avg(response_time_s), p95 = percentile(response_time_s, 95) by service_code_s, persona_s

Key Takeaways

  • Hub-spoke VNet topology · APEX-M spoke peers to a hub holding shared services.
  • Per-Service subnet pattern: container-apps · private-endpoints · gateway · management.
  • STAGE+ requires private endpoints for all Microsoft platform resources; private DNS zones per service type.
  • One Log Analytics workspace per environment is the single pane: App Insights + Sentinel + Container Apps + Fabric + KeyVault + Entra signals all converge.
  • The on-call's six daily KQL queries cover Service health, cost, grounding drift, incidents, capacity throttling, and HITL responsiveness.
Chapter 13

Day-Zero Tenant Onboarding

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Sequences the actions that stand up the APEX Platform in a brand-new client Azure tenant.
  • Splits client-side actions from Deloitte-side actions — the Independence boundary made operational.
  • Validates the Platform with a smoke test before any Service is added.
60-second read
  • Day-Zero is sequenced: client prerequisites → Bicep what-if → Layer 1 deploy → smoke test → handoff.
  • Five client-side prerequisites must tick before Deloitte runs Bicep — they instantiate client ownership of the Microsoft platform.
  • Smoke test confirms all seven Microsoft resource types are live · Defender is scanning · Notation verifies · Container App revisions deploy · Microsoft.KeyVault HSM crypto operates.
  • Handoff artifact is the Tenant Profile YAML — every subsequent Service onboarding consumes it.
  • Hands-on walkthrough of all of this lives in Ch 17 — Day-Zero in Practice.

13.1 The Sequence at a Glance

  1. Client-side prerequisites — subscription, Microsoft Entra ID delegation, Microsoft Defender enablement, deployment-principal role assignment.
  2. Bicep dry run — az deployment sub what-if against the client subscription. Review and approve the resource plan with the client architect.
  3. Layer 1 deployment — az deployment sub create with the Sprint 1 main.bicep; emits the seven Microsoft resource types from §4.1.
  4. Smoke test — confirm Layer 1 outputs are live, sign a test image into the new container registry, verify the chain Microsoft Notation policy.
  5. Handoff — record the deployment in Microsoft Cost Management tag taxonomy, register the tenant in the Service catalog.

The full step-by-step is in Appendix B; this chapter is the narrative.

Client action Deloitte action Microsoft platform response

13.2 Client-Side Prerequisites (Five Boxes That Must Tick First)

Before a Deloitte deployment principal touches the client subscription, the client must complete five actions. None of them is technical heavy lifting; all of them are structural prerequisites of the two-contract model.

  1. Provision the Azure subscription under the client's Enterprise Agreement. Microsoft platform consumption flows under the client's MS contract.
  2. Enable Microsoft Defender for Cloud at subscription scope, including Microsoft Defender for Containers, Microsoft Defender for Key Vault, Microsoft Defender for App Service.
  3. Create a deployment service principal in Microsoft Entra ID and grant it scoped permissions: Contributor at resource-group level, plus a custom role that allows Microsoft.KeyVault/vaults/keys/write on the future Key Vault. Federated credential preferred over secret-based.
  4. Enable Microsoft Cost Management tag inheritance at subscription scope, with the apex:* tag set in the inherited list.
  5. (If applicable) Enable Microsoft Sentinel as a workspace solution — to be attached after Layer 1's Log Analytics workspace exists.
Independence — Why these are client-side

Each of the five prerequisites instantiates client ownership of the platform. The subscription is the client's; the Defender posture is the client's regulatory choice; the deployment principal is the client's gatekeeper for Deloitte access. If Deloitte performs any of these, the structural separation slips. Day-Zero will not begin until all five are confirmed.

13.3 The Bicep Dry Run

With prerequisites green, the Deloitte deployment principal authenticates into the client subscription and runs the what-if. Two shells are equally supported — Azure CLI and Az PowerShell — both call the same Microsoft.Resources REST API.

SHELL — what-if before deploy (Azure CLI / bash)
az login --service-principal \
   -u $DEPLOY_SP_APP_ID \
   --federated-token "$FEDERATED_TOKEN" \
   --tenant $CLIENT_TENANT_ID

az account set --subscription "$CLIENT_SUBSCRIPTION_ID"

az deployment sub what-if \
   --location centralus \
   --template-file MVP/agentic-merch/bicep/main.bicep \
   --parameters MVP/agentic-merch/bicep/parameters/<client>-lab01.bicepparam
POWERSHELL — what-if before deploy (Az PowerShell)
Connect-AzAccount `
    -ServicePrincipal `
    -TenantId       $env:CLIENT_TENANT_ID `
    -ApplicationId  $env:DEPLOY_SP_APP_ID `
    -FederatedToken $env:AZURE_FEDERATED_TOKEN

Set-AzContext -Subscription $env:CLIENT_SUBSCRIPTION_ID

Get-AzSubscriptionDeploymentWhatIfResult `
    -Location          'centralus' `
    -TemplateFile      'MVP\agentic-merch\bicep\main.bicep' `
    -TemplateParameterFile 'MVP\agentic-merch\bicep\parameters\<client>-lab01.bicepparam'

The what-if output is reviewed with the client architect, line by line. Every Microsoft resource type from §4.1 should appear as + Create. Any unexpected ~ Modify or - Delete halts the deploy.

Note — PowerShell deployment cookbook

Appendix H contains the complete line-level PowerShell command set for Day-Zero (§H.3), per-Service onboarding (§H.4), image mirroring (§H.5), Microsoft Notation verify (§H.6), Microsoft.KeyVault operations (§H.7), Container Apps canary & traffic-split (§H.8), smoke testing (§H.9), LEDGER chain verification (§H.10), and rollback (§H.11). Use it as a copy-paste cookbook when the delivery shell is PowerShell.

13.4 Layer 1 Deployment

On approval, the same command with create instead of what-if runs the deployment. Typical wall-clock for the Sprint 1 module set: 12–18 minutes — Premium container registry provisioning is the slowest single resource at ~6 minutes; Microsoft.KeyVault Premium HSM key creation is ~2 minutes; Container Apps Environment is ~4 minutes; everything else is sub-minute.

13.5 Smoke Test

Layer 1 is "live" when:

  • All seven Microsoft resource types from §4.1 are in Succeeded state in the resource group.
  • Microsoft Defender for Containers shows the new container registry registered for scanning.
  • A test image (e.g., apex-acr.azurecr.io/agent-base:1.0.0) imports into the client container registry and verifies under the client's Notation trust policy.
  • A no-op Container App revision deploys, exposes /healthz, returns 200, and emits a trace to Microsoft.Insights/components.
  • Microsoft.KeyVault crypto operations work: a sign with the CMK returns a valid signature.

If any item fails, do not proceed to Service onboarding. Layer 1 has to be unambiguously green.

13.6 Handoff Artifacts

Day-Zero closes with a single artifact: the Tenant Profile file, checked into the delivery repo, listing:

YAML — tenant profile
tenant_code: contoso-na
client_tenant_id: 11111111-2222-3333-4444-...
client_subscription_id: aaaa-bbbb-cccc-dddd-...
deployment_suffix: lab01
location: centralus
platform_version: 0.1.0
microsoft_defender:
  for_cloud: enabled
  for_containers: enabled
  for_key_vault: enabled
sentinel: pending     # to be enabled at STAGE
purview_account: contoso-purview-na
acr_login_server: apexacrlab01.azurecr.io
keyvault_uri: https://apexkvlab01.vault.azure.net/
log_analytics_workspace_id: /subscriptions/.../workspaces/apex-law-lab01
container_apps_env_id: /subscriptions/.../managedEnvironments/apex-env-lab01
managed_identities:
  runtime: /subscriptions/.../mi-apex-merch-runtime-lab01
  mcp:     /subscriptions/.../mi-apex-merch-mcp-lab01
  ingest:  /subscriptions/.../mi-apex-merch-ingest-lab01
  pii_unlock: /subscriptions/.../mi-apex-merch-pii-unlock-lab01

Every subsequent Service onboarding into this tenant consumes the Tenant Profile as input. Without it, Service deployment cannot reference Layer 1.

Key Takeaways

  • Day-Zero is sequenced: client prerequisites → Bicep what-if → Layer 1 deploy → smoke test → handoff.
  • Five client-side prerequisites must tick before Deloitte runs Bicep — they instantiate client ownership of the Microsoft platform.
  • Smoke test confirms all seven Microsoft resource types are live, Defender is scanning, Notation verifies, Container App revisions deploy, Microsoft.KeyVault HSM crypto operates.
  • Handoff artifact is the Tenant Profile YAML — every subsequent Service onboarding consumes it.

Deployment Drills

  1. Run a Day-Zero against your Lab subscription using a fresh deployment suffix. Time each phase. Identify the slowest Microsoft resource and whether it is on the critical path.
  2. Walk Appendix B's checklist with the role of "client architect." Note any line item that requires Deloitte authority to confirm — those are Independence-boundary smells.
  3. Open the Tenant Profile of an existing Lab deployment. Verify every resource ID resolves with az resource show.
Chapter 13.5

Pre-deployment Security Gate

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Defines the operator-facing checklist that every APEX-M tenant deployment must pass before Bicep apply.
  • Maps every checklist item to the Microsoft control that satisfies it (Defender for Cloud / Defender for AI / Purview / Entra Agent ID / Customer Lockbox / AI Model Security).
  • Cites the Microsoft Cloud Security Benchmark v2 (AI Security baseline) controls each item ties to.
60-second read
  • The wizard's render endpoint refuses to emit Bicep parameters until every gate item is green for the target tenant.
  • Items split into structural (must be true forever) and per-deployment (must be true for this wave).
  • Microsoft Defender for Cloud CSPM with AI security posture, Defender for AI services, Microsoft Purview Information Protection + Audit, Microsoft Entra Agent ID, and AI Model Security scan are all required pre-prod.

13.5.3 Operator workflow

The wizard's /security-gate page (Phase I.7 follow-up) renders this checklist with live-state per gate, polled from Defender for Cloud + Purview + Entra. Each gate has three states: green, yellow (action recommended), red (blocks render). The wizard's render endpoint refuses to emit Bicep parameters when any gate is red.

Operators MUST attach evidence (Defender posture report PDF, Purview labels report, AI Model Security scan output) before approving the gate. Evidence lands in the engagement's audit trail.

13.5.4 What this chapter is NOT

  • It is not a substitute for client information-security review. Client's CISO chain reviews APEX-M deployment posture independently; this gate is the tenant SRE's pre-flight check.
  • It is not a substitute for the Independence consultation per Deloitte's Microsoft Technology & Services Practice. Item #14 is the gate; the consultation is the substance.
  • It is not optional for production tenants. Lab and Dev substrates may waive items 7, 8, 13 with documented sign-off. Stage and Prod must satisfy every item.

Key Takeaways

  • 14 gate items split between structural (one-time per tenant) and per-deployment (per wave); every Stage/Prod tenant must satisfy all.
  • Wizard render refuses to emit Bicep parameters when any gate is red.
  • Each gate cites the Microsoft control and the Microsoft Cloud Security Benchmark v2 AI Security baseline control id.
  • Independence consultation is gate #14 — Deloitte Independence Office signs off per adapter in scope.
Chapter 14

Adding a Service to a Tenant

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Walks the per-Service onboarding motion — identical for the first Service and the fortieth.
  • Shows how the Tenant Profile from Day-Zero feeds the Service Bicep.
  • Validates the Service against its KPI envelope through the test harness before traffic flows.
60-second read
  • Service onboarding is an eight-step loop: Tenant Profile → image mirror → secrets → what-if → deploy → harness → SOR (PROD) → promote.
  • The test harness gates promotion regardless of Bicep success — Bicep validates resources · harness validates behavior.
  • Time budget: ~75 min for the first Service · ~55 min for each subsequent — caching is the architecture's reward.

14.1 The Service Onboarding Loop

For any Service from Appendix E being added to a tenant whose Layer 1 is live:

  1. Confirm the Tenant Profile exists and Layer 1 version satisfies the Service's platform_compatible range.
  2. Mirror agent images the Service composes from the Deloitte authoring registry to the tenant container registry. Each az acr import brings bytes + Microsoft Notation signatures + cosign attestations.
  3. Materialize Service-scoped secrets — Microsoft.KeyVault/vaults/secrets entries with <service-code>-* prefix.
  4. Run Service Bicep what-if against the tenant subscription, parameterized by the Tenant Profile + agent SHA digests + Service version.
  5. Deploy Service Bicep on approval. Microsoft.App/containerApps come up; Microsoft.Storage / Microsoft.DocumentDB / Microsoft.Sql per-Service stores come up.
  6. Run the test harness against the deployed Service — UC catalog drives end-to-end validation through the deployed Microsoft.App/containerApps endpoints.
  7. Connect SOR (in PROD only) — the Service's bronze landing connects to the client's source-of-record systems via Microsoft Fabric mirroring or Microsoft.DataFactory pipelines.
  8. Promote — the Service becomes the active envelope; KPI tracking begins; HITL personas are notified.

14.2 The Test Harness as Acceptance Gate

The MVP test harness in MVP/TestHarness/ is a contract validator. It loads the UC catalog (TestHarness/catalog_seed/uc_catalog.json), and for each UC, runs a multi-step scenario against the Service's Container Apps. Each step writes to LEDGER; the harness verifies the chain at end of run.

For RC-E2E-03, the harness validates UCs that exercise:

  • The two scenarios in scope (cold-chain excursion, dynamic markdown).
  • The five-agent composition (every agent sees an inbound /run in some UC).
  • HITL approval loop — the Operations Lead's adaptive-card output survives a round-trip through the harness's HITL stub.
  • LEDGER hash chain integrity at end-of-UC.

If the harness reports any UC FAIL, the Service is not promoted. The harness gates the rollout regardless of whether the Bicep deployed successfully.

Best Practice — Harness against deployed Service

Run the harness against the freshly deployed Microsoft.App/containerApps endpoints, not against laptop containers. The whole point of the dual-substrate architecture is that the harness exercises the same behavior on either substrate; running against deployed Service catches Microsoft platform-specific issues (managed-identity auth, Microsoft.KeyVault secret resolution, Microsoft Notation pull-time verification) that laptop runs cannot.

14.3 Service Onboarding Time Budget

PhaseFirst Service in tenantSubsequent Services
Tenant Profile validation5 min2 min
Image mirror (5 agents)10 min5 min (most images cached)
Service-scoped secret materialization3 min3 min
Bicep what-if + review15 min5 min
Bicep deployment12 min10 min
Test harness run (full UC catalog)30 min30 min
SOR connection (PROD only)2–3 days1–2 days
Total (DEV/STAGE, no SOR)~75 min~55 min

Subsequent Services are cheaper not because of any procedural shortcut but because of caching: agent images already in the tenant container registry, the Tenant Profile already built, the harness already configured. The architecture causes the cost curve to fall.

Key Takeaways

  • Service onboarding is an eight-step loop: Tenant Profile → image mirror → secrets → what-if → deploy → harness → SOR (PROD) → promote.
  • The test harness gates promotion regardless of Bicep success — Bicep validates resources, harness validates behavior.
  • Time budget: ~75 min for the first Service, ~55 min for each subsequent — caching is the architecture's reward.

Deployment Drills

  1. Run a Service onboarding against your Lab Tenant Profile. Time each phase. Compare against §14.3.
  2. Pick a UC from the harness catalog. Trace its end-to-end execution path through deployed Microsoft.App/containerApps endpoints.
  3. For one Service in Appendix E that you have not yet onboarded, sketch the per-Service secret list and the agent-image mirror list.
Chapter 15

Agent Upgrades Across Clients

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Walks the upgrade motion when a Layer 3 agent image needs to roll out across many Services and many tenants.
  • Maps the canary-and-promote pattern using Microsoft.App/containerApps revisions and traffic-split rules.
  • Establishes the CAB / approval cadence aligned to the three-layer SemVer.
60-second read
  • Agent upgrades are byte-level changes; what they trigger depends on patch / minor / major SemVer bump.
  • Within a tenant: Microsoft.App/containerApps revision-multi mode + traffic-split for canary 5% → 25% → 50% → 100%.
  • Cross-tenant rollout is a wave plan: Lab → STAGE → early-PROD → all-PROD; each wave gate produces an audit-replicable checkpoint.
  • Don't fan-out a digest to all tenants in one shot; per-tenant canary protects against tenant-specific regressions.

15.1 What "Agent Upgrade" Means at Each Layer

An agent upgrade is, at the byte level, a new SHA digest in the Deloitte authoring registry. What it triggers depends on whether the bump is patch, minor, or major:

  • Patch — prompt tweak, weight retune. Agents within compatible Service version ranges pick up the new digest on next mirror + revision rollover. No Service Bicep change.
  • Minor — new MCP tool dependency, new env var, backward-compatible behavior change. Service Bicep parameter file updates the SHA digest; the Service version may patch-bump.
  • Major — HTTP contract change at /run, LEDGER row schema change. Service version major or minor bumps; compatibility matrix updates; client-tenant rollout follows the upgrade window.

15.2 The Canary-and-Promote Pattern

Microsoft.App/containerApps supports multiple revisions per app with traffic-split rules. The standard canary pattern for an agent upgrade in a single tenant:

  1. Mirror the new agent SHA digest to the tenant container registry (az acr import).
  2. Update the Service Bicep parameter file with the new digest. Run Bicep with revision-mode set to multiple.
  3. The new revision deploys alongside the current revision. Traffic-split is set to 95% current / 5% new initially.
  4. Observe Microsoft.Insights for ~30 minutes. Compare cost-per-decision, safety-verdict distribution, LEDGER row counts between revisions.
  5. If healthy, ramp: 75/25 → 50/50 → 0/100. If unhealthy, snap back to 100/0 on previous revision.
  6. Once at 0/100 stable for 24 hours, retire the previous revision.
BICEP — traffic split for canary
configuration: {
  activeRevisionsMode: 'Multiple'
  ingress: {
    external: false
    targetPort: 8000
    transport: 'http'
    traffic: [
      { revisionName: '${appName}--rev1-4-0', weight: 95 }
      { revisionName: '${appName}--rev1-4-1', weight: 5,  latestRevision: false }
    ]
  }
}

15.3 Cross-Tenant Rollout

Across multiple client tenants, the same canary motion runs per tenant in waves. Typical wave plan for a Layer 3 minor upgrade:

  1. Wave 0 — Lab v0.1 (DEV). Full canary + observation. Pass criterion: 24 hours stable + harness PASS.
  2. Wave 1 — STAGE in 1–2 client tenants. Synthetic load + smoke harness.
  3. Wave 2 — PROD in early-adopter tenants. Canary + 72-hour observation per tenant.
  4. Wave 3 — PROD in remaining tenants. Standard canary; rollback playbook on file.

Each wave gate is a small CAB-equivalent — not a process for its own sake but a checkpoint the audit chain can later reconstruct ("on date X, version Y was promoted to wave Z based on observability evidence E").

Warning — Don't roll the same digest into all tenants in one shot

It is technically possible to mirror an agent image to all client tenant registrys at once and then redeploy Service Bicep across all tenants in parallel. It is not advisable. The first tenant catches issues that the laboratory could not — the canary-per-tenant cadence protects against tenant-specific regressions in Microsoft.KeyVault HSM region, Microsoft Defender policy version, or Microsoft Sentinel rule interaction.

Key Takeaways

  • Agent upgrades are byte-level changes; what they trigger depends on patch / minor / major SemVer bump.
  • Within a tenant, use Microsoft.App/containerApps revision-multi mode + traffic-split for canary 5% → 25% → 50% → 100%.
  • Cross-tenant rollout is a wave plan: Lab → STAGE → early-PROD → all-PROD; each wave gate produces an audit-replicable checkpoint.
  • Don't fan-out a digest to all tenants in one shot; per-tenant canary protects against tenant-specific regressions.

Deployment Drills

  1. Configure Microsoft.App/containerApps in your Lab to run two revisions of one agent at 95/5. Watch the Microsoft.Insights traffic split for 30 minutes.
  2. Write the wave-plan template for a hypothetical upgrade of The Analyst 1.4.0 → 1.5.0 across three tenants. Define each wave's pass criteria.
  3. For one prior upgrade, reconstruct the audit narrative from LEDGER + Microsoft.Insights — confirm a regulator could rebuild the rollout decision from primary sources.
Chapter 16

Rollback, DR, Reusable-Asset Discipline

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Establishes the rollback motion at each of the three layers — what reverses, what does not.
  • Maps the Microsoft.RecoveryServices and Microsoft.DataProtection posture for DR of LEDGER and Service state.
  • Closes the book by stating the reusable-asset discipline that lets the next client onboarding cost less than the last.
60-second read
  • Rollback is layer-asymmetric: agents in seconds (traffic-split snap-back) · Services in ~10 minutes (Bicep redeploy) · Platform in 30+ minutes — LEDGER is append-only across all.
  • DR uses Microsoft-native primitives: geo-replicated Microsoft.ContainerRegistry · zone-redundant Microsoft.App/managedEnvironments · geo-redundant Microsoft.Sql · Microsoft.RecoveryServices and Microsoft.DataProtection vaults.
  • The reusable-asset discipline — no client-specific Bicep forks · no :latest tags · no per-client harness rewrites · no out-of-band runbooks — is the only thing that makes the cost curve fall over time.
  • Independence boundaries — two contracts · two container registries · no shared ECIF — are enforced architecturally.

16.1 Rollback by Layer

LayerRollback mechanismReversal time
3 (Agent)Microsoft.App/containerApps traffic-split snap back to prior revision SHASeconds
2 (Service)Bicep redeploy of prior Service version + parameter file from git~10 min
1 (Platform)Bicep redeploy of prior Layer 1 version; some resources non-destructive (Microsoft.KeyVault soft-delete + restore), some require recreate30+ min

The crucial property: rollback at any layer leaves the LEDGER intact. Audit chain rows about the failed upgrade are still in the chain; they are not deleted, only superseded by subsequent rows reverting the change. This is a Microsoft.Sql / Microsoft.DBforPostgreSQL behavior with append-only enforcement at the LEDGER table level — a structural rather than procedural guarantee.

16.2 What Cannot Be Rolled Back

  • Microsoft.KeyVault HSM key deletion — Layer 1 enables purge protection; the soft-deleted key recovers within 90 days. Past that, key material is unrecoverable. Operationally this means a CMK rotation must overlap with the prior key for at least 90 days before the prior key is purged.
  • LEDGER rows — WORM. Append only. Rollback produces a new row that supersedes; it does not erase.
  • Microsoft.ContainerRegistry image deletion — once a digest is removed from the registry and from any cached pull, the bytes are gone. Sprint 1's retention policy keeps 30 days of all images, plus indefinitely-retained signed-and-promoted versions.
  • Client-tenant data plane — STAGE/PROD bronze landing is the client's record; Deloitte does not own the rollback there.

16.3 DR Posture

Disaster recovery for the APEX deployment uses Microsoft-native DR primitives:

ResourceDR mechanismRTO target
Microsoft.ContainerRegistryGeo-replication (Premium SKU) — image bytes available in paired region within seconds< 5 min
Microsoft.KeyVault PremiumRegion-replicated by default; CMK accessible from any region< 5 min
Microsoft.App/managedEnvironmentsZone-redundant; recreate in paired region from Bicep on regional failure30–60 min
Microsoft.Sql / Microsoft.DBforPostgreSQL (LEDGER)Geo-redundant backups + active geo-replication15 min
Microsoft.Storage (Service bronze/silver)RA-GRS; client owns SOR resyncPer Service
Microsoft.DocumentDB (Cosmos DB session)Multi-region writes optional; default read-replica failover5 min
Microsoft.OperationalInsightsWorkspace data retained per region; recreate from Bicep on cross-region failover15 min

Microsoft.RecoveryServices vaults back up the Microsoft.Sql LEDGER and select Microsoft.Storage accounts. Microsoft.DataProtection backup vaults cover Microsoft.KeyVault and Microsoft.DocumentDB. These are Layer 1 concerns — Day-Zero (Chapter 13) provisions them when the substrate tier is STAGE or PROD.

16.4 The Reusable-Asset Discipline

The closing argument of the book: the deployment cost per client falls only if every artifact built for client N is reusable for client N+1. The MVP is the reusable asset; this guide is the discipline.

The discipline, in practice, is four habits:

  1. Resist client-specific Bicep forks. If a client requires a deviation, parameterize it. If parameterization is not possible, push the deviation into a Service-level module, never a Layer 1 module.
  2. Resist tag-pinning to latest. Every agent image reference is a SHA digest. Every. One.
  3. Resist re-implementing the harness per client. The UC catalog is generic. Client-specific scenarios extend it; they do not replace it.
  4. Resist out-of-band documentation. If a client deployment needs an instruction that is not in this book or its sources (deployment.md, the Service envelopes, Appendix E), update the source. The book is the operating manual; if it does not say something, the deployment cannot rely on it.

Each habit is unglamorous. Each habit, kept, makes the fortieth client deploy in a day what the first client took a quarter to deploy. That is the entire commercial story of why APEX is a reusable asset and not a custom build per engagement — and it is also the only durable reason a delivery team gets to use this book as a checklist instead of writing one from scratch.

Independence — Closing reminder

Across all sixteen chapters, the relationship is described as "Deloitte's Microsoft practice," "DMTSP," "Microsoft platform capabilities," or "Microsoft-native deployment." Never partner, never alliance, never strategic alliance. The two-contract commercial structure — client ↔ Microsoft for platform, client ↔ Deloitte for services, ECIF direct — is enforced architecturally by the boundaries described in Chapter 1 (two container registries), Chapter 3 (subscription separation), and Chapter 13 (client-side prerequisites). If you find yourself drafting a client-facing artifact that erodes any of those boundaries, the deployment posture has slipped before the words have. Restate the boundaries and rebuild the artifact.

Key Takeaways

  • Rollback is layer-asymmetric: agents in seconds, Services in minutes, Platform in tens of minutes — LEDGER is append-only across all.
  • DR uses Microsoft-native primitives: geo-replicated Microsoft.ContainerRegistry, zone-redundant Microsoft.App/managedEnvironments, geo-redundant Microsoft.Sql, Microsoft.RecoveryServices and Microsoft.DataProtection vaults.
  • The reusable-asset discipline — no client-specific Bicep forks, no :latest tags, no per-client harness rewrites, no out-of-band runbooks — is the only thing that makes the cost curve fall over time.
  • Independence boundaries — two contracts, two container registries, no shared ECIF — are enforced architecturally; if the artifact slips, the architecture has slipped.

Deployment Drills

  1. Run a rollback exercise on your Lab: revert the latest Service Bicep deploy via traffic-split + parameter-file revert. Time to back-to-stable.
  2. Audit the last three engagement artifacts for client-specific deviations. Identify any that should have been parameterized into the reusable Bicep instead.
  3. Open the reusable-asset discipline four habits and grade your delivery org's adherence honestly. Schedule the corrective work for whichever habit scores lowest.
Part VI

Practitioner Tracks

Day 91. Marisol approved her first real markdown card yesterday — the Wave-1 commitment is met. The Service is in production. The platform is humming. Priya could close her laptop and start prep for the next engagement. But two things matter now that she's past Day-Zero: operating what she built (the on-call rotation begins), and teaching the next Priya who will inherit a similar engagement and a similar timeline.

Part VI is the practitioner's hands-on companion. Chapter 17 is the linear walkthrough — from git clone at hour zero to a verified Layer-1 deployment in a Lab Azure subscription. It's the chapter you give the new joiner who needs to feel the deployment with their hands before they read everything that came before. Chapter 18 goes deep on CI/CD and FinOps — the four artifact streams that promote together (Bicep · image SHAs · Fabric items · Entra/Purview scripts), the cost-per-decision telemetry, the burn-rate alerts that catch budget drift before it shows up in next quarter's P&L. Chapter 19 covers Day-2 operations and engineering standards — the on-call SLA, the incident-response playbook, the change-management cadence, the coding standards and review discipline that the engagement passes to the managed-services partner at Wave-1 → Wave-2 handover.

Read Part VI as both reference and tutorial. New joiners read Chapter 17 in order. Sustain-motion leaders read Chapter 19 in order. CFOs and engagement leads jump to Chapter 18 for the cost story. The four appendices (Microsoft resource cookbook · PowerShell deployment cookbook · the Glossary · the Diagrams Library · the End-to-End Runbook) carry the reference detail the practitioner needs at 3am.

  • Chapter 19Day-2 Operations & Engineering Standards
Chapter 16.5

Failure Scenarios & Chaos Engineering

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Catalogues APEX-M's twelve canonical failure modes with detection · graceful-degrade behavior · recovery procedure.
  • Establishes a chaos-engineering practice using Azure Chaos Studio (Microsoft.Chaos/experiments) and APEX-M's per-Service chaos profiles.
  • Extends Ch 15's DR/Rollback with RPO/RTO targets per substrate component and the paired-region failover playbook.
60-second read
  • Twelve canonical failure classes: model deployment unavailable · model timeout · grounding-source unreachable · KV unlock denied · MCP tool quota · Eventstream backlog · Fabric capacity throttling · A2A peer down · Container Apps replica crash · LEDGER write block · Sentinel rule storm · region-wide outage.
  • RPO/RTO targets: LEDGER RPO = 0 (synchronous) · Bronze RPO ≤ 15min · Silver RPO ≤ 1h · Gold RPO ≤ 4h · agent fleet RTO ≤ 5min · LEDGER RTO ≤ 15min · workspace RTO ≤ 4h.
  • Azure Chaos Studio runs scheduled experiments in STAGE (never PROD) injecting each failure class once a quarter; results land in LEDGER + Sentinel.
  • Paired-region failover via Azure Front Door + Bicep parameterised on region pair (e.g., East US 2 + West US 3).

16.5.1 The Twelve Failure Classes

#FailureDetectionGraceful degradeRecovery
1Foundry model deployment unavailable5xx from FoundryChatClient · capacity exhaustedCost-envelope router falls back to lighter model · agent flags in audit rowWait for capacity · scale up Foundry quota · or shift to paired region
2Model call timeoutLatency > 30s · client cancelRetry with exponential backoff (3 attempts) · then escalate to HITL with "agent unavailable"Capacity scale-up · model swap
3Grounding source unreachable (Gold mart query fails)MCP tool 5xx · timeoutAgent emits "data unavailable · recommend manual review" · HITL adaptive cardRestore Fabric workspace · re-run Silver conformance
4KV unlock denied (Tier-3 PHI)403 from pii-unlock identityBlock · log to LEDGER · alert pii-unlock approvers · operator sees redacted formRe-validate dual-control · re-issue unlock
5MCP tool quota exceeded429 from MCP hostQueue request · retry after backoff windowScale Container App replicas · raise quota
6Eventstream backlogLag > 5min · backpressure to sourceEventhouse buffers · downstream Activator delays · operator sees "trigger delayed" indicatorScale Eventstream throughput · investigate downstream consumer
7Microsoft.Fabric capacity throttlingCapacity Metrics App alert · CU rejectionForeground (agent calls) prioritized · background jobs throttledBurst absorbed by smoothing · sustained → scale SKU
8A2A peer downA2A endpoint returns 503Caller agent collapses to in-process function-tool fallback if possible · else HITLRestart peer's Container App · or wait for paired-region failover
9Container Apps replica crashHealth probe fail · K8s livenessAuto-restart · traffic shifts to healthy replicasImage SHA rollback · investigate crash dump
10LEDGER write blockKV write fail · WORM blob 503Agent blocks all decisions — no audit row, no commitHighest-priority recovery; LEDGER is the trust anchor
11Sentinel rule stormAlert volume > thresholdAutomation rules dedupe · top-N incidents onlyTune rules · review root cause (was it real or a misconfigured detection?)
12Region-wide outageMultiple resources unreachableFront Door redirects to paired region · agents come up therePaired-region failover playbook (§16.5.3)

16.5.2 Azure Chaos Studio Bicep

infra/bicep/platform/chaos.bicep · STAGE only
resource chaosTarget 'Microsoft.Chaos/targets@2024-01-01' = {
  scope: containerApp
  name: 'microsoft-azure-containerapps'
  properties: {}
}

resource killReplicaCapability 'Microsoft.Chaos/targets/capabilities@2024-01-01' = {
  parent: chaosTarget
  name: 'KillReplica-1.0'
}

resource experiment 'Microsoft.Chaos/experiments@2024-11-01-preview' = {
  name: 'chaos-${engagementCode}-monthly'
  location: location
  identity: { type: 'SystemAssigned' }
  properties: {
    steps: [
      {
        name: 'Step1-Kill-Pricer-Replica'
        branches: [{
          name: 'Branch1'
          actions: [{
            type: 'continuous'
            name: 'urn:csci:microsoft:azureContainerApps:killReplica/1.0'
            duration: 'PT5M'
            selectorId: 'thePricerSelector'
          }]
        }]
      }
    ]
    selectors: [{
      id: 'thePricerSelector'
      type: 'List'
      targets: [{ type: 'ChaosTarget'; id: chaosTarget.id }]
    }]
  }
}

16.5.3 Paired-Region Failover Playbook

  1. Front Door health probe detects primary region 503; auto-shifts traffic to paired region
  2. Foundry project in paired region (cold standby; image pre-pulled) warms up
  3. Microsoft.Fabric — workspace must be re-created in paired region; OneLake shortcut pre-configured from primary; data eventually replicates · operate read-only in failover region initially
  4. LEDGER — KV with geo-replicated CMK · WORM blob is RA-GZRS · failover anchors continue uninterrupted
  5. Microsoft.Cache/Redis Premium replicates passively; manual promote on failover
  6. Microsoft.DocumentDB (Cosmos) — multi-region write-enabled in PROD for zero-downtime

Per-component RPO/RTO documented in the Compliance Posture Document (Services Appendix L).

Key Takeaways

  • Twelve canonical failure modes have documented detection · graceful-degrade · recovery patterns.
  • LEDGER write block is the only failure that blocks decisions entirely; everything else degrades to HITL with operator visibility.
  • Azure Chaos Studio runs scheduled experiments in STAGE only; PROD relies on real-event learning + paired-region failover.
  • Paired-region failover is RPO/RTO-budgeted per component; Front Door redirects traffic; LEDGER continuity is the highest-priority constraint.
Chapter 17

Day-Zero in Practice — A First-Tenant Walkthrough

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Walks a fresh laptop and a Lab Azure subscription through a complete Layer 1 Day-Zero deployment in nine sequenced phases.
  • Concretizes every concept in Chapter 13 against real az CLI / Az PowerShell commands — copy, paste, run.
  • Gives every chapter that came before a touchpoint — when reading those chapters again, the reader has a working tenant to refer to.
60-second read
  • One half-day · ~3 hours of wall-clock · ends with a verified Layer 1 Platform deployment in a Lab Azure subscription, ready to receive Service onboardings.
  • Phases 0–3 are client-side prerequisites (subscription · Defender · deployment principal · tags). Phases 4–8 are the Deloitte-side Bicep motion (login · what-if · deploy · smoke test · Tenant Profile handoff).
  • Every command is shown in both Azure CLI (bash) and Az PowerShell so the reader's preferred shell works.
  • The chapter mirrors the eight-hour Services Guide §26 walkthrough but at the Layer 1 Platform tier; together they take the reader from empty repo to working RC-E2E-03 in two sittings.

17.0 What You Need Before You Start

  • A Lab Azure subscription you have rights to · or a sandbox under the Deloitte authoring tenant.
  • Azure CLI (az ≥ 2.60) · Az PowerShell module (Az ≥ 11.0) · Bicep CLI (≥ 0.30) · git · your editor of choice.
  • Microsoft Entra ID account with Contributor on the subscription · or the ability to ask the subscription owner to create a deployment service principal.
  • The Deployment Guide repo cloned at MVP/agentic-merch/bicep/.

17.1 Phase 0 — Confirm the Lab Subscription Is Ready

Phase 0 is a five-minute check that the prerequisite client-side actions from §13.2 are in place. In Lab, you may be both the "client" and the "Deloitte deployment principal," so this phase tests both halves of the boundary at once.

SHELL — confirm subscription readiness
az login
az account set --subscription "$LAB_SUBSCRIPTION_ID"

# Confirm Microsoft Defender for Cloud is enabled at subscription scope
az security pricing list --query "[?name=='Containers' || name=='KeyVaults' || name=='AppServices'].{Resource:name, Tier:pricingTier}"

# Confirm tag inheritance · expect 'Enabled' or set it
az config set defaults.location=centralus

# Verify you can create a resource group (the Day-Zero target)
az group list --query "[?starts_with(name,'rg-apex-merch-')]"

If Microsoft Defender for Containers shows Free or Off, enable it before going further (Appendix B Day-Zero checklist item 2):

SHELL — enable Defender for Containers / Key Vault / App Service
az security pricing create --name Containers --tier Standard
az security pricing create --name KeyVaults  --tier Standard
az security pricing create --name AppServices --tier Standard

17.2 Phase 1 — Create the Deployment Service Principal

In production this is a federated service principal owned by the client (§3.4 · §13.2 prerequisite 3). In Lab you can create a simpler SP under your own tenant for the walkthrough.

SHELL — create deployment SP
# Create the SP and grant Contributor on the future resource group's parent (subscription)
SP_OUTPUT=$(az ad sp create-for-rbac \
    --name "sp-apex-deploy-lab" \
    --role Contributor \
    --scopes "/subscriptions/$LAB_SUBSCRIPTION_ID")

echo "$SP_OUTPUT"
# Save the output (appId · password · tenant) somewhere secure for the next phase.
# In production, replace this with federated-credential setup; never store the password.

17.3 Phase 2 — Author the Tenant Parameter File

Per §13.3, every Day-Zero needs a Tenant-specific parameter file that overrides defaults in main.bicep. Author it now:

BICEPPARAM — parameters/lab01.bicepparam
using '../main.bicep'

param location          = 'centralus'
param deploymentSuffix  = 'lab01'
param tags = {
  'apex:layer'         : 'platform'
  'apex:tenant-suffix' : 'lab01'
  'apex:owner'         : 'deloitte-mvp-engineering'
  'apex:environment'   : 'lab'
}

For STAGE/PROD the parameter file would also pin a VNet ID, private-endpoint subnet, and Log-Analytics retention; in Lab the defaults are sufficient.

17.4 Phase 3 — Run Bicep What-If

Per §13.3, run what-if before any deployment. Inspect every resource the Bicep is about to create.

SHELL — what-if (Azure CLI)
az deployment sub what-if \
    --location centralus \
    --template-file MVP/agentic-merch/bicep/main.bicep \
    --parameters MVP/agentic-merch/bicep/parameters/lab01.bicepparam
POWERSHELL — what-if (Az PowerShell)
Get-AzSubscriptionDeploymentWhatIfResult `
    -Location          'centralus' `
    -TemplateFile      'MVP\agentic-merch\bicep\main.bicep' `
    -TemplateParameterFile 'MVP\agentic-merch\bicep\parameters\lab01.bicepparam'

Expected output: 7 resource types in + Create state (Microsoft.ContainerRegistry · 4× Microsoft.ManagedIdentity · Microsoft.KeyVault · Microsoft.KeyVault/keys · Microsoft.OperationalInsights · Microsoft.Insights · Microsoft.App/managedEnvironments). No ~ Modify, no - Delete. If you see anything else, halt and read the diff.

17.5 Phase 4 — Deploy Layer 1

Run the deployment. Wall-clock is 12–18 minutes; Premium ACR provisioning is the longest single resource at ~6 minutes.

SHELL — Layer 1 deploy
DEPLOYMENT_NAME="apex-platform-$(date +%Y%m%d-%H%M)"

az deployment sub create \
    --name "$DEPLOYMENT_NAME" \
    --location centralus \
    --template-file MVP/agentic-merch/bicep/main.bicep \
    --parameters MVP/agentic-merch/bicep/parameters/lab01.bicepparam

# Watch the deployment in a separate terminal:
az deployment sub show --name "$DEPLOYMENT_NAME" --query "properties.provisioningState"

While it runs, this is a good moment to re-read Chapter 4 with the actual resource list in mind. By the time you finish §4.7 (Layer 1 outputs), the deployment usually has finished too.

17.6 Phase 5 — Verify the Seven Resources Are Live

SHELL — verify Layer 1 resources
az resource list --resource-group "rg-apex-merch-lab01" \
    --query "[].{Name:name, Type:type, Location:location}" --output table

Expected output: a table with seven rows matching §4.1 — the Premium ACR, four user-assigned managed identities, the Premium Key Vault with the CMK, the Log Analytics workspace, the Application Insights component, and the Container Apps managed environment. If anything is missing, walk back through the deployment operations:

SHELL — investigate failed operations
az deployment operation sub list --name "$DEPLOYMENT_NAME" \
    --query "[?properties.provisioningState=='Failed'].{Resource:properties.targetResource.id, Status:properties.statusMessage.error.message}"

17.7 Phase 6 — Smoke Test the Platform

Per §13.5 / Appendix B.4, the smoke test confirms the platform is functionally live before any Service is added.

SHELL — smoke test
# 1. Microsoft Defender for Containers shows ACR registered for scanning
az security assessment list --query "[?contains(resourceDetails.id,'apexacrlab01')]" --output table

# 2. Test image imports into ACR
az acr import --name apexacrlab01 \
    --source mcr.microsoft.com/azuredocs/aci-helloworld:latest \
    --image hello-world:smoketest

az acr repository list --name apexacrlab01 --output table

# 3. Microsoft.KeyVault HSM CMK exists
az keyvault key show --vault-name apexkvlab01 --name apex-merch-cmk \
    --query "{name:key.kid, type:key.kty, ops:key.keyOps}"

# 4. Container Apps environment is healthy
az containerapp env list --resource-group "rg-apex-merch-lab01" \
    --query "[].{Name:name, State:properties.provisioningState}"

All four checks should return clean. If Microsoft Defender shows the ACR not yet registered, give it 5 minutes — the registration runs asynchronously after ACR provisioning.

17.8 Phase 7 — Capture the Tenant Profile

The Tenant Profile YAML is the handoff artifact that every subsequent Service onboarding consumes (§13.6 · Ch 14). Generate it from the deployment outputs:

SHELL — emit Tenant Profile
OUTPUTS=$(az deployment sub show --name "$DEPLOYMENT_NAME" --query "properties.outputs")

cat > "MVP/delivery/tenant-profiles/lab01.yaml" <<EOF
tenant_code: deloitte-lab
client_tenant_id: $(az account show --query tenantId -o tsv)
client_subscription_id: $LAB_SUBSCRIPTION_ID
deployment_suffix: lab01
location: centralus
platform_version: 0.1.0
acr_login_server: $(echo "$OUTPUTS" | jq -r '.acrLoginServer.value')
keyvault_uri:     $(echo "$OUTPUTS" | jq -r '.keyvaultUri.value')
cmk_key_uri:      $(echo "$OUTPUTS" | jq -r '.cmkKeyUri.value')
log_analytics_workspace_id: $(echo "$OUTPUTS" | jq -r '.logAnalyticsWorkspaceId.value')
container_apps_env_id:      $(echo "$OUTPUTS" | jq -r '.containerAppsEnvId.value')
app_insights_connection_string: $(echo "$OUTPUTS" | jq -r '.appInsightsConnectionString.value')
managed_identities:
  runtime: $(echo "$OUTPUTS" | jq -r '.runtimeIdentityId.value')
  mcp:     $(echo "$OUTPUTS" | jq -r '.mcpIdentityId.value')
EOF

# Commit the Tenant Profile to the delivery repo
git -C MVP add delivery/tenant-profiles/lab01.yaml
git -C MVP commit -m "Day-Zero handoff: lab01 Tenant Profile"

17.9 Phase 8 — What's Next

You now have a verified Layer 1 Platform in your Lab subscription, ready to receive its first Service. Three good directions tomorrow:

  1. Onboard RC-E2E-03 as your first Service. Read Ch 14 as the procedure; consume the lab01.yaml Tenant Profile from Phase 7 as the input. Time budget: ~75 minutes for the first Service onboarding (§14.3).
  2. Run the Services Guide §26 hands-on walkthrough. The eight-hour Services Guide chapter takes you from docker compose up through a verified LEDGER chain — but it works on the laptop tier. Today's chapter is the Azure-tier counterpart. Run both, see how the same Service behaves on each substrate.
  3. Read Appendix I (Anti-Patterns & Pitfalls). With a working Layer 1 in front of you, the anti-patterns become concrete — you can see why each one would have broken your deployment if you'd done it.

17.10 If a Phase Fails — The Diagnostic Tree

PhaseIf it failsFirst investigation
0 subscriptionDefender prices show "Free"Subscription owner needs to enable; in Lab, az security pricing create
1 SP creationInsufficient privilegesMicrosoft Entra ID admin needs to grant Application Developer or App Registration role
2 parameter fileBicep can't find .bicepparamPath relative to main.bicep · ensure using statement is correct
3 what-ifResource conflictsAn existing resource group with the same name exists; choose a different deploymentSuffix
4 deployMicrosoft.KeyVault provisioning failsSubscription likely has soft-deleted key vault with the same name; az keyvault list-deleted and recover or rename
5 verifyResource missing from listCheck az deployment operation sub list for the failure; cross-ref §H.7 (Microsoft Fabric capacity exhaustion · rare but possible at Lab tier)
6 smokeDefender doesn't show ACR scannedWait 5 minutes · the registration is async; if still missing, manual az security assessment trigger
7 Tenant ProfileDeployment outputs emptyDeployment didn't actually succeed despite phase 4 not erroring · re-check provisioningState · re-run if needed

Key Takeaways

  • Half a day · zero wasted hours · ends with a verified Layer 1 Platform ready for its first Service onboarding.
  • Every phase exercises a concept from Chapters 4–13 — the architecture is now concrete, not abstract.
  • The Tenant Profile YAML at the end is the contract every subsequent Service deployment consumes.
  • Pair this chapter with Services Guide §26 (laptop tier) and Ch 14 (Service onboarding) for the full end-to-end motion.

Deployment Drills

  1. Repeat Phases 1–8 with a different deploymentSuffix (e.g., lab02) and confirm two independent Layer 1 Platforms can coexist in the same subscription.
  2. After Phase 8, hand the Tenant Profile YAML to a colleague and have them onboard a Service against it. Time them; compare to the §14.3 budget.
  3. Roll back the entire Day-Zero by deleting the resource group · then redeploy. Confirm the Microsoft.KeyVault soft-delete recovery works (Ch 16 §16.2 · Appendix H.6 in the Services Guide).
Chapter 18

CI/CD & FinOps — Deep Dive

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Walks the APEX-M CI/CD pipeline coordinating four parallel artifact streams: Bicep · agent images · Fabric definitions · Entra/Purview config scripts.
  • Establishes the FinOps practice — per-Service cost attribution · capacity-unit budgeting · cost-per-decision telemetry · burn-rate alerts.
60-second read
  • Four artifact streams promote together: Bicep templates · agent container images · Fabric items (semantic models · pipelines · notebooks) · Entra/Purview config scripts. One commit ⇒ one coordinated promotion.
  • Per-environment GitHub Actions or Azure DevOps pipeline per Service · OIDC federation to per-Service deployment SP · no client secrets.
  • Cost attribution via Azure tags on every resource: apex.engagement · apex.service · apex.practice · apex.tier.
  • Cost-per-decision telemetry emits to LEDGER per agent invocation; Power BI dashboard surfaces it daily.
  • Burn-rate alerts at 50% / 80% / 100% of monthly Cost envelope per Service · Microsoft Cost Management Budgets resource type.

18.1 The Four Artifact Streams

StreamRepo pathPromotion mechanism
Bicepinfra/bicep/azd provision + az deployment sub create per stage; Bicep what-if pre-commit
Agent imagesapps/agents/<agent>/docker buildx linux/amd64 → push to Microsoft.ContainerRegistry → SHA promotion across stages (no rebuild)
Microsoft Fabric itemsapps/services/<svc>/fabric/Git integration on workspace + deployment pipeline (per §6A.12)
Entra/Purview configdeployment-scripts/*.ps1Invoked from Bicep deploymentScripts with platform UAMI; idempotent

18.2 GitHub Actions Pipeline Example

.github/workflows/promote-stage-to-prod.yml
name: promote-stage-to-prod
on:
  workflow_dispatch:
    inputs:
      serviceCode:
        description: 'Service to promote (e.g., rc-e2e-03)'
        required: true

permissions: { id-token: write; contents: read }

jobs:
  preflight:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: azure/login@v2
        with:
          client-id: ${{ vars.PROD_DEPLOY_CLIENT_ID }}
          tenant-id: ${{ vars.TENANT_ID }}
          subscription-id: ${{ vars.PROD_SUBSCRIPTION_ID }}
      - name: Verify STAGE eval gate green
        run: pwsh ./pipelines/check-eval-gate.ps1 -ServiceCode ${{ inputs.serviceCode }} -Tier stage
      - name: Verify cosign attestation
        run: cosign verify "${ACR_NAME}.azurecr.io/apex-m/${{ inputs.serviceCode }}:${IMAGE_SHA}"
      - name: Verify Compliance Posture Document signed
        run: pwsh ./pipelines/check-cpd-signed.ps1 -ServiceCode ${{ inputs.serviceCode }}

  promote-bicep:
    needs: preflight
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: azure/login@v2
        with:
          client-id: ${{ vars.PROD_DEPLOY_CLIENT_ID }}
          tenant-id: ${{ vars.TENANT_ID }}
          subscription-id: ${{ vars.PROD_SUBSCRIPTION_ID }}
      - run: az deployment sub create
              --location eastus2
              --template-file infra/bicep/blueprints/w1-foundation.bicep
              --parameters @infra/bicep/parameters/${{ inputs.serviceCode }}-prod.json

  promote-images:
    needs: promote-bicep
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - name: Promote image SHA (no rebuild)
        run: pwsh ./pipelines/promote-image-sha.ps1
              -ServiceCode ${{ inputs.serviceCode }}
              -FromStage stage -ToStage prod

  promote-fabric:
    needs: promote-bicep
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - name: Run Fabric deployment pipeline
        run: pwsh ./deployment-scripts/fabric-pipeline.ps1 -ServiceCode ${{ inputs.serviceCode }} -Stage Production

  promote-entra-purview:
    needs: promote-bicep
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - name: Apply Entra config
        run: pwsh ./deployment-scripts/conditional-access-baseline.ps1 -EngagementCode prod
      - name: Apply Purview config
        run: pwsh ./deployment-scripts/sensitivity-labels.ps1 -EngagementCode prod
      - name: Apply DLP/IRM policies
        run: pwsh ./deployment-scripts/dlp-baseline.ps1 -EngagementCode prod

18.3 Cost Tagging Baseline

Every Bicep resource carries four required tags. Azure Policy enforces; deployments without all four are rejected:

infra/bicep/main.bicep · required tag block
var requiredTags = {
  'apex.engagement': engagementCode
  'apex.service': serviceCode
  'apex.practice': practiceCode    // rc · hls · er · axle · th · tmt · ice · shared
  'apex.tier': tier                // lab · dev · stage · prod
  'apex.cost-center': costCenterCode
  'apex.cpd-version': cpdVersion   // Compliance Posture Document version
}

// Apply to every resource via tags: requiredTags

18.4 Cost-per-Decision Telemetry

Every agent invocation emits a LEDGER row capturing model-call cost. Aggregated daily by a Microsoft Fabric Notebook into a Gold mart consumed by Power BI Direct Lake (per Services Guide §29.5):

Cost-per-decision calculation (per LEDGER row)
cost_per_invocation = sum(
  prompt_tokens     × $/Mtok input  (per model deployment)
  completion_tokens × $/Mtok output (per model deployment)
  + tool_call_count × $/MCP-call (negligible · for completeness)
)

cost_per_decision = sum(cost_per_invocation across all agents that participated)

// Decision = the operator-approved or auto-committed action,
// not a single agent call. A Magentic workflow with 5 agent calls
// produces ONE decision with cost = sum of 5 invocations.

18.5 Budgets & Burn-Rate Alerts

infra/bicep/platform/budgets.bicep · per-Service monthly budget
resource budget 'Microsoft.Consumption/budgets@2024-08-01' = {
  scope: resourceGroup()
  name: 'budget-${serviceCode}-${tier}-monthly'
  properties: {
    category: 'Cost'
    amount: monthlyBudgetUsd
    timeGrain: 'Monthly'
    timePeriod: { startDate: budgetStartDate }
    filter: {
      tags: { name: 'apex.service'; operator: 'In'; values: [serviceCode] }
    }
    notifications: {
      Burn-50: { enabled: true; operator: 'GreaterThan'; threshold: 50; contactEmails: ['apex-finops@contoso.com'] }
      Burn-80: { enabled: true; operator: 'GreaterThan'; threshold: 80; contactEmails: ['apex-finops@contoso.com', 'apex-engagement-lead@contoso.com'] }
      Burn-100: { enabled: true; operator: 'GreaterThan'; threshold: 100; contactEmails: ['apex-engagement-lead@contoso.com', 'apex-engagement-partner@contoso.com'] }
    }
  }
}

Key Takeaways

  • Four artifact streams promote together via one Service-level pipeline: Bicep · images · Fabric items · Entra/Purview scripts.
  • OIDC federation between GitHub Actions / Azure DevOps and per-stage deploy SPs · no client secrets.
  • Pre-promotion preflight: eval gates green · cosign attestation verified · Compliance Posture Document signed.
  • Six required tags on every resource enforce cost attribution; Azure Policy blocks deploys missing them.
  • Cost-per-decision emitted to LEDGER per invocation · aggregated to Gold · surfaced in Power BI Direct Lake.
  • Budgets at per-Service granularity with 50/80/100 burn-rate alerts escalate up the chain.
Chapter 19

Day-2 Operations & Engineering Standards

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Defines the APEX-M Day-2 sustain motion: on-call rotation · incident response · change management · capacity scaling cadence · agent retraining cycles.
  • Codifies engineering standards — coding style · testing pyramid · code review · security review gate · documentation requirements — that every APEX-M engagement applies.
60-second read
  • Day-2 motion: on-call rotation (24×7 follow-the-sun for PROD) · incident response with severity-tiered SLA · monthly change windows · quarterly capacity review · agent retrain cycle (typically quarterly · governed by eval drift).
  • Engineering standards: Python = ruff + mypy strict · .NET = StyleCop + Roslyn analyzers · TDD with three test tiers (unit · integration · golden-set) · two reviewers per PR · security review gate for any Service Bicep change.
  • Documentation: per-Service README · model card · Compliance Posture Document · operational runbook · architecture-decision-record (ADR) for every non-trivial decision.

19.1 On-Call Rotation & SLA

SeverityExamplesAcknowledge SLAResolution target
Sev 1 — criticalLEDGER write block · Tier-3 PII breach · region-wide outage15 min2h (with progress comms every 30min)
Sev 2 — highAgent identity high-risk · single-Service unavailable · grounding-score collapse30 min4h
Sev 3 — mediumCapacity throttling · MCP tool error rate > 5% · cost-envelope burn 80%2h (business hours)1 business day
Sev 4 — lowSingle-agent transient error · stale Power BI refresh · model-card content gap1 business day1 sprint

19.2 Incident Response Playbook

  1. Detect — Sentinel alert or manual report · auto-opens Sentinel incident
  2. Triage — on-call validates · assigns severity · pages additional teams per playbook
  3. Containment — execute the matching SOAR playbook (per §6B.4) · stop the bleeding
  4. Investigation — Microsoft Security Copilot natural-language KQL · trace LEDGER hash-chain · review DSPM-for-AI activity
  5. Recovery — apply fix · validate · close incident · update LEDGER with resolution row
  6. Post-incident review — within 5 business days · blameless · output: runbook update · detection rule update · golden-set case · ADR if structural

19.3 Change Management

  • Standard change (pre-approved) — image SHA promote · sensitivity-label tweak · KQL rule update · Bicep parameter change. Deploy any business hour.
  • Normal change — new Service · new agent · new MCP tool · model swap. Change Advisory Board approval (engagement-lead + service-architect + compliance-lead). Deploy in monthly change window.
  • Emergency change — security fix · regulatory directive · production hotfix. Engagement-lead approval (single approver acceptable) · post-hoc CAB review.

19.4 Capacity Scaling Cadence

  • Weekly — review Fabric Capacity Metrics App + Container App scaling history + cost-per-decision dashboard
  • Monthly — formal capacity review · adjust scale rules · plan upcoming Service additions
  • Quarterly — re-baseline Cost envelope per Service · Foundry model deployment audit · capacity SKU review

19.5 Agent Retraining Cycle

  • Continuous — golden-set additions from operator-reported issues
  • Per-sprint — prompt tweaks (prompt_version bump · re-eval before promotion)
  • Quarterly — model-deployment audit · candidate model swap if Cost envelope or capability profile shifts · full golden-set regression on new model
  • Triggered — eval drift > threshold · operator-reject rate > threshold · grounding-score collapse

19.6 Engineering Standards

AreaStandard
Python styleruff + mypy in strict mode · pyproject.toml at repo root · pre-commit enforced
.NET styleStyleCop + Roslyn analyzers · .editorconfig at repo root
Bicep stylebicep-linter with project preset · what-if mandatory pre-merge
Testing pyramid(70%) unit (pytest / xUnit) · (20%) integration (against Lab v0.1) · (10%) golden-set regression (Foundry batch eval)
Code review2 reviewers minimum · 1 must be service-architect for Service changes · CODEOWNERS enforced
Security reviewRequired for any Bicep change touching identity / KV / public endpoint / new Service · security-lead sign-off in PR review
Commit messagesConventional Commits · type(scope): summary · refs to issue · ADR reference where applicable
Branchingmain = trunk · feature branches short-lived · no long-running release branches
Secret handlingNo secrets in code · no secrets in CI variables · all via Microsoft.KeyVault + OIDC federation
DocumentationPer-Service: README · model card · operational runbook · CPD · ADRs for non-trivial decisions

19.7 Sustain Motion Handover Checklist

At Wave 1 → Wave 2 transition (per Sellers Guide §31.5), delivery hands over to managed-services. Required artifacts:

  • Operational runbook (per failure class · per Service) — Services Appendix H
  • Compliance Posture Document (signed current version) — Services Appendix L
  • On-call rotation populated (named individuals · escalation tree)
  • Sentinel detections + SOAR playbooks operating in PROD enforce mode
  • Budgets + burn-rate alerts active
  • Per-Service golden set sized + LLM-as-judge sampling enabled
  • Three months of canary metric history (baseline for drift detection)
  • Daily KQL dashboard pinned in Sentinel + Power BI
  • Sustain partner contract signed (managed-services agreement separate from delivery SOW)

Key Takeaways

  • Day-2 motion has four cadences: continuous (golden-set + telemetry) · weekly (capacity / cost review) · monthly (change window · normal CAB) · quarterly (capacity baseline · model audit).
  • Incident response is severity-tiered with Sentinel + Security Copilot as primary tooling; blameless post-incident review feeds runbooks, detections, and ADRs.
  • Engineering standards are non-negotiable: ruff/mypy/StyleCop · TDD with 3 test tiers · 2 reviewers + service-architect + security-lead for Bicep · OIDC + KV for all secrets.
  • Wave 1 → Wave 2 handover requires the eight artifacts in §19.7 before sustain partner accepts the engagement.
Appendix I

Anti-Patterns & Pitfalls

The reusable-asset discipline (§16.4) lists four habits to keep. This appendix lists the inverse — the deployment mistakes we have actually seen break engagements, organized by where they typically occur. Each entry has a name (so teams can call it out by name during code review), a symptom, the signal that surfaces it, and the corrective action. The list is not exhaustive; contributing new entries by following the pattern is encouraged.

I.1 Layer-Mixing Anti-Patterns

Anti-pattern · "Service Bicep creates its own ACR"

Symptom: A Service Bicep module includes a Microsoft.ContainerRegistry/registries resource block instead of consuming the Layer 1 ACR via parameter input.

Signal: What-if shows a new ACR being created when deploying the Service. Cost report shows multiple ACRs in the same tenant.

Why it breaks: Image deduplication is lost across Services. Layer 3 image promotion has to mirror to N registries instead of one. Layer 1 amortization (§9.1) collapses; per-Service cost rises with N.

Fix: Remove the ACR block from the Service Bicep. Add acrLoginServer as an input parameter consuming the Layer 1 output. The Layer 1 ACR is the only ACR in the tenant.

Anti-pattern · "Service module duplicates managed identities"

Symptom: A Service Bicep creates new Microsoft.ManagedIdentity/userAssignedIdentities resources instead of consuming the Layer 1 four-identity set.

Signal: Microsoft Entra ID enterprise applications inflate with per-Service runtime/MCP/ingest identities. Conditional-access policies become unmanageable.

Why it breaks: The four-identity blast-radius split (§4.3) is undermined. Compromise containment becomes per-Service rather than per-function.

Fix: Remove the identity creation from the Service Bicep. Consume runtimeIdentityId, mcpIdentityId, etc. as parameters from the Tenant Profile.

Anti-pattern · "Per-tenant agent build"

Symptom: Agent images are built from source in each client tenant's CI pipeline rather than mirrored from the Deloitte authoring registry.

Signal: Tenant ACRs show az acr build task runs. Image SHA digests differ between tenants for the "same" agent version.

Why it breaks: The two-registry Independence boundary collapses (§1.4 · §3.3.1). Image bytes are no longer identical across tenants; cosign attestations don't propagate. Audit reproducibility ("what code ran on day X") fragments per tenant.

Fix: Build only in the Deloitte authoring registry. Mirror via az acr import. SHA digests must match across all tenant registries holding the same agent version.

I.2 Image-Reference Anti-Patterns

Anti-pattern · "Tag-pinned image reference"

Symptom: A Service Bicep references agent images by tag (e.g., agent-the-analyst:1.4.0) rather than by SHA digest.

Signal: Container Apps revision template shows image: ...:1.4.0 instead of image: ...@sha256:....

Why it breaks: Tags are mutable on the registry side. A re-tagged image will silently pick up new bytes on the next replica scale-out. The Service deployment's audit narrative ("this Bicep deployed these specific agent bytes on date X") becomes unprovable.

Fix: Always reference by digest. The promotion pipeline (Appendix D) writes the digest into the Service parameter file; the parameter file is checked in; the audit trail is git-replicable.

Anti-pattern · ":latest in production"

Symptom: Anywhere — Bicep, compose file, ARM template — a Container App pulls image:latest.

Signal: Grep for :latest in the deployment repo finds matches outside of explicitly laptop-only docker-compose files.

Why it breaks: Same as tag-pinning, but worse — there is no reproducibility at all. Container App revisions become time-of-day-dependent.

Fix: Block in CI. Reject any deployment artifact that contains :latest outside the laptop-tier compose file (where it's acceptable for dev convenience but not production).

I.3 Identity & Secrets Anti-Patterns

Anti-pattern · "API key in env var"

Symptom: A Container App has plain-text API keys, connection strings, or service-principal passwords in its env: block (rather than secretRef-resolved Microsoft.KeyVault references).

Signal: Bicep diff shows raw secret values; or a leaked credential scan finds keys in the deployment history.

Why it breaks: The whole identity model from Ch 5 is bypassed. Microsoft Entra ID conditional access doesn't apply. Compromise containment fails.

Fix: Move the secret to Microsoft.KeyVault. Reference by secretRef with the appropriate identity binding (runtime · MCP · pii-unlock per §5.2.1). Rotate the previously-exposed credential.

Anti-pattern · "PII unlock identity bound at deploy time"

Symptom: The mi-apex-merch-pii-unlock identity appears in a Container App's identity: block as a standing user-assigned identity.

Signal: Bicep grep finds piiUnlockIdentityId wired into a Container App resource definition.

Why it breaks: The just-in-time pattern from §5.2.2 is the entire security argument for splitting pii-unlock from runtime. Standing-bind gives every replica permanent unwrap permission.

Fix: Remove pii-unlock from the Container App's standing identity list. Acquire JIT via acquireTokenForUserAssignedIdentity at the HITL approval moment, with a LEDGER row written before the unwrap fires.

I.4 Network Anti-Patterns

Anti-pattern · "Public-network access in PROD"

Symptom: PROD Microsoft.ContainerRegistry, Microsoft.KeyVault, or Microsoft.Sql shows publicNetworkAccess: 'Enabled'.

Signal: Bicep audit run finds public access flags in PROD parameter files.

Why it breaks: The §6.2 STAGE/PROD posture (private endpoints + VNet integration + NSGs) collapses. Microsoft Defender for Cloud will flag it; Microsoft Sentinel UEBA will alert; the audit committee will note it.

Fix: Set publicNetworkAccess: 'Disabled' in PROD parameter files. Add private endpoints. Run the Bicep audit pre-commit hook to keep it from regressing.

I.5 Audit & Compliance Anti-Patterns

Anti-pattern · "LEDGER UPDATE/DELETE allowed"

Symptom: The LEDGER table in Azure SQL Database or Microsoft.DBforPostgreSQL Flexible Server allows UPDATE or DELETE operations from the runtime identity.

Signal: Database-level role grants show UPDATE/DELETE on LEDGER for non-break-glass identities.

Why it breaks: The append-only WORM enforcement (§16.2 · cross-ref Services Guide §25.2) is structural, not procedural. Without DB-level revocation, a runtime bug or compromised identity can rewrite history.

Fix: REVOKE UPDATE, DELETE ON LEDGER FROM <runtime_role>. Only a break-glass admin role retains UPDATE/DELETE; use of break-glass triggers Microsoft Sentinel UEBA alert.

Anti-pattern · "Tags missing on deployed resources"

Symptom: Microsoft Cost Management reports show resources without apex:service-code or apex:layer tags.

Signal: Cost-by-tag query returns "untagged" rollup > 5% of total spend.

Why it breaks: The W3 outcome-based commercial envelope requires per-Service cost attribution (§9.2 · cross-ref Services Guide Ch 29). Untagged resources break the Service P&L query.

Fix: Audit Bicep tag inheritance. Add apex:layer · apex:service-code · apex:tenant-suffix as mandatory tags in main.bicep and propagate to every tags: block. Re-tag deployed resources via az resource tag if needed.

I.6 Operational Anti-Patterns

Anti-pattern · "Skip-the-canary upgrade"

Symptom: An agent image upgrade is rolled to 100% traffic in a tenant immediately, without a 5%/25%/50% canary ramp (§15.2).

Signal: Container App revision history shows traffic split going from 100/0 on the prior revision directly to 0/100 on the new revision.

Why it breaks: Tenant-specific regressions (Microsoft.KeyVault HSM region differences, Microsoft Sentinel rule interaction, Microsoft Defender policy version mismatch) are caught only at the canary stage. Skip-the-canary masks regressions until they hit production traffic at scale.

Fix: Enforce the canary motion in the deployment pipeline. Block PRs that bypass traffic-split staging. Pair with golden-set + LLM-as-judge evaluation gates from Services Guide §28.5.

Anti-pattern · "All tenants in one shot"

Symptom: An agent image upgrade is mirrored to all client tenant ACRs at once and Service Bicep redeployed across all tenants in parallel.

Signal: Wave plan for the upgrade has fewer than three waves, or all waves complete within hours.

Why it breaks: Same as skip-the-canary, but at the cross-tenant scale. The first tenant catches issues that the laboratory could not — per-tenant canary protects against tenant-specific regressions in HSM region · Defender policy · Sentinel rule interaction.

Fix: Wave plan from §15.3 — Lab v0.1 (Wave 0) · STAGE in 1–2 client tenants (Wave 1) · PROD early-adopter tenants (Wave 2) · all PROD tenants (Wave 3). Each wave's pass criterion: 24-hour observation + harness PASS.

I.7 Independence Anti-Patterns

Anti-pattern · "Deloitte ACR pulled at runtime by client tenant"

Symptom: A client tenant Container App pulls images from apex-acr.azurecr.io (the Deloitte authoring registry) at runtime instead of from the per-tenant ACR.

Signal: Container App configuration shows registries: [{server: 'apex-acr.azurecr.io', ...}] in production.

Why it breaks: The structural enforcement of the two-contract model at Layer 3 (§1.4 · §3.3.1) is undone. Client deployments now have a runtime dependency on a Deloitte-owned subscription. Independence is performatively asserted but structurally broken.

Fix: All client Container Apps reference the per-tenant ACR. The Deloitte authoring ACR is reachable only by the mirror import path, never by runtime pull.

Anti-pattern · "Partner / alliance language in deployment artifacts"

Symptom: Bicep parameter descriptions, README files, runbooks, or operator-facing docs contain partner, alliance, or strategic alliance.

Signal: Regex grep across the delivery repo finds matches.

Why it breaks: The Independence posture (§3.2 · cross-ref Sellers Guide §6.13) is a contractual structure. Artifacts surviving the engagement that erode the language can be quoted back at procurement reviews three years later.

Fix: Pre-commit hook with the regex as a build-fail. Bulk replace existing matches with DMTSP · Microsoft platform capabilities · Microsoft-native deployment. Verify clean on every PR.

I.8 The Pre-Commit Hook (Recommended)

Most of these anti-patterns are catchable by automated pre-commit checks. Recommend wiring the following into the delivery repo's .git/hooks/pre-commit:

SHELL — pre-commit anti-pattern checks
#!/usr/bin/env bash
set -e

# 1. Block partner/alliance language
if git diff --cached | grep -iE 'partner|strategic alliance|alliance' > /dev/null; then
  echo "✗ Independence violation: partner/alliance language found"; exit 1
fi

# 2. Block :latest in non-laptop files
if git diff --cached --name-only | grep -v 'docker-compose' | xargs grep -l ':latest' 2>/dev/null; then
  echo "✗ Tag-pinning violation: :latest found outside laptop-tier compose"; exit 1
fi

# 3. Block public-network access in PROD parameter files
if git diff --cached --name-only | grep -E 'parameters/.*-prod\.bicepparam$' | xargs grep -l "publicNetworkAccess.*Enabled" 2>/dev/null; then
  echo "✗ Network-posture violation: PROD has publicNetworkAccess=Enabled"; exit 1
fi

# 4. Block secret values in env blocks (heuristic: long alphanumeric strings)
if git diff --cached -- '*.bicep' | grep -E "value:.*[A-Za-z0-9+/]{40,}" > /dev/null; then
  echo "✗ Secret-in-env warning: long string in Bicep env value · review for secrets"; exit 1
fi

echo "✓ Pre-commit anti-pattern checks passed"
Appendix A

Bicep Module Reference

Sprint 1 of the MVP shipped five Bicep modules under MVP/agentic-merch/bicep/modules/ plus a top-level main.bicep. This appendix is the quick reference for parameters, outputs, and Microsoft API versions.

A.1 main.bicep

Subscription-scope deployment. Imports five modules; emits the Tenant Profile fields consumed by every Layer 2 Service Bicep.

BICEP — main.bicep parameter list
targetScope = 'subscription'

param location          string = 'centralus'
param deploymentSuffix  string = 'lab01'
param resourceGroupName string = 'rg-apex-merch-${deploymentSuffix}'
param tags object = {
  'apex:layer':         'platform'
  'apex:tenant-suffix': deploymentSuffix
  'apex:owner':         'deloitte-mvp-engineering'
}

resource rg 'Microsoft.Resources/resourceGroups@2023-07-01' = {
  name: resourceGroupName
  location: location
  tags: tags
}

module identity      'modules/identity.bicep'      = { ... }
module keyvault      'modules/keyvault.bicep'      = { ... }
module acr           'modules/acr.bicep'           = { ... }
module observability 'modules/observability.bicep' = { ... }
module agents        'modules/agents.bicep'        = { ... }

output runtimeIdentityId           string = identity.outputs.runtimeIdentityId
output runtimePrincipalId          string = identity.outputs.runtimePrincipalId
output mcpIdentityId               string = identity.outputs.mcpIdentityId
output keyvaultUri                 string = keyvault.outputs.kvUri
output cmkKeyUri                   string = keyvault.outputs.cmkKeyUri
output acrLoginServer              string = acr.outputs.loginServer
output containerAppsEnvId          string = agents.outputs.envId
output logAnalyticsWorkspaceId     string = observability.outputs.logAnalyticsWorkspaceId
output appInsightsConnectionString string = observability.outputs.appInsightsConnectionString

A.2 Per-Module Reference

ModuleMicrosoft resources deployedKey parametersKey outputs
acr.bicepMicrosoft.ContainerRegistry/registries (Premium) · Microsoft.Authorization/roleAssignments (AcrPull)location, deploymentSuffix, runtimeIdentityId, runtimePrincipalId, cmkKeyUriloginServer, registryId
identity.bicepMicrosoft.ManagedIdentity/userAssignedIdentities (×4)location, deploymentSuffixruntimeIdentityId, runtimePrincipalId, mcpIdentityId, ingestIdentityId, piiUnlockIdentityId
